Abstract: Educational simulations are a well-established pedagogical technique that gives learners opportunities to apply knowledge, respond to challenges, and reflect on their performance. High-quality simulations, however, are difficult to build, requiring instructor input, scenario design, character development, scaffolding, technical implementation, and repeated revision. This paper presents Primer, an open-source, LLM-agnostic AI-supported system that helps educators create adaptive educational simulations. Educators contribute their knowledge of the subject, lesson, and students, while generative AI produces responsive variations of practice for students. Primer uses a multi-agent system centered around executing educator's instructional intent through the entire learning experience. We hope that by releasing Primer as an open-source system, including its prompts and GitHub repository, we provide a foundation for development and research on educator-in-the-loop AI authoring, multi-agent educational workflows, and simulation-based pedagogy. Further, by allowing the use of various AI models, including local and open source models, we provide the opportunity for implementation under varied governance regimes. Primer points toward a model of educational AI in which instructors can create locally grounded practice opportunities based on their own materials, goals, examples, and knowledge of students.

</details>

Abstract: Coding agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in software development, yet their ability to tackle long-horizon engineering tasks governed by physical constraints remains less understood. We study this question through analog circuit layout, which demands domain knowledge, geometric reasoning, and iterative interaction with design and verification tools. We introduce \textbf{AnalogLayoutWorld}, an open-source agent benchmark with 40 expert-reviewed tasks for end-to-end layout generation from fixed transistor-level netlists. It combines an integrated design environment with independent physical and post-layout electrical verification, as well as objective layout quality metrics co-designed with analog layout experts. Across 13 model--harness configurations, 91.1\% of attempts produce GDS layouts, but only 23.0\% satisfy the benchmark's acceptance criteria. We also evaluate Novara, an internally developed agent system, which achieves the highest reported aggregate score. Our analysis links performance differences to test-time scaling under fixed budgets, suggesting that reasoning efficiency and effective use of verification feedback help sustain design quality as task complexity grows.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents use external skills to reuse task procedures and experience with tools. General-purpose skills describe shared execution procedures, while specific tool choices, operating practices, and output requirements vary across users. In long-horizon tasks, subgoals, available inputs, and environmental states change as execution progresses. Agents must select currently applicable skills from an expanding skill pool and prepare candidates for subsequent steps.
  We propose Skill Adapter, which enables personalized modification and online routing of general-purpose skills. The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.
  We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Compared with SkillRL, it improves task success rates by an average of 11.43 percentage points and reduces mean execution time on all three benchmarks. Skill Adapter retains its performance advantage when the skill pool grows to 1,000 skills.

</details>

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models are pretrained on thousands of hours of recordings, yet their frozen features transfer poorly to downstream tasks, and this transfer does not consistently improve with larger models or more pretraining data. We show that a vision foundation model that has never seen EEG is a stronger frozen EEG encoder than these specialized models, once the signals are rendered as images that a vision transformer can read. Our method, SeeEEG, keeps the vision backbone frozen and learns only how EEG is displayed to it. Each channel occupies a dedicated row of patches and is drawn either as a waveform at a fixed gain or as a scalogram whose frequency range spans the patch height, so that task-relevant patterns fall within individual tokens. The montage, filtering, and contrast parameters of this display are initialized from a standard clinical montage and optimized with the task loss through the frozen backbone, which amounts to at most 1,113 trainable parameters per view. We compare SeeEEG with seven EEG foundation models on eight clinical, sleep, affective, and brain-computer interface datasets, with every encoder frozen and paired with identical readout heads. With a frozen DINOv3 ViT-B, SeeEEG outperforms every EEG foundation model in balanced accuracy on all eight datasets and reaches a mean rank of 1.3 in Cohen kappa or AUROC, compared with 2.9 for the strongest baseline, REVE. The learned displays transfer without further optimization to the video encoder V-JEPA 2.1, which outperforms every EEG foundation model on both metrics on six of the eight datasets. Replacing DINOv3 with a randomly initialized ViT-B lowers the score by 15.2 points on average, so the ability to read rendered EEG comes from visual pretraining.

</details>

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation systems attach citations to attribute claims to their sources. These citations frequently misstate provenance, and standard verification cannot detect this. We measure provenance by counterfactual value substitution: a fact in the retrieved passages is replaced with a false variant and the model re-queried. We find that across five sizes of one model family and a second comparator family, 1.2% to 15.7% of citable claims derive from parametric memory rather than the retrieved context. At the most conservative substitution distance these figures are 4.6% and 8.5% on the two models we tested at it, with the rate rising with model size within a family. We further find that natural language inference verification, the standard approach, gives results indistinguishable from chance across models, even when restricted to the cited passage. We determine that the cause is structural: a model answering from memory selects a citation that agrees with its output. Comparing ten attribution methods on identical claims, the contrast between the model's distribution with and without context best separates provenance. Acting on that signal does not improve provenance honesty at these base rates. The resulting detector is hence still not accurate enough to act on: it measures what the model knew rather than what it used, and separating those remains the open problem.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables independent data owners to train models collaboratively without centralizing raw data. However, identifying collaborators, preparing training tasks, and coordinating execution and incentives hinder its practical adoption by organizations without specialized expertise. We present Cortex-FL, an agent-native FL system that enables users to express learning objectives in natural language and delegate the collaboration required to train a model. Agents translate these objectives into shared tasks, discover collaborators, and negotiate participation agreements, connecting collaboration formation with distributed training and incentive management. The architecture separates agent coordination from local execution authority, allowing owners to delegate coordination while retaining control over their data, computation, and financial commitments. We demonstrate the complete workflow on a four-node deployment, in which a model requested in natural language proceeds through collaboration formation, contract-governed training, verified model delivery, and x402 settlement on a blockchain testnet.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents Al Ghasali, a computational framework designed to formalize and simulate Islamic jurisprudential (Fiqh) rulings. Building upon recent set theoretic formalizations of al sabr wa al Taqsim that extract minimal operational rules as simple Horn clause logical programs, this work proves that the semantics of closed Fiqh chapters collapse completely to deterministic Finite State Automata (FSA). We establish a formal computational hierarchy, demonstrating that restricting formalization to finite propositional Horn theories guarantees decidability and constant time (O(1)) verdict extraction after offline compilation. The framework formally proves that hierarchical compositions of sub-chapter FSAs preserve this O(1) complexity, and that temporal juristic requirements, such as Tartib (order), are deterministically flattened into static ontological states, preserving the memoryless property of the automaton. To generate human-readable juristic summaries, Al Ghasali incorporates Large Language Models (LLMs) via an architecture where the deterministic FSA strictly controls the probabilistic LLM. Crucially, the paper formulates sufficient design safety conditions for this FSA LLM integration, ensuring strict adherence to the epistemological boundaries of Usul al Fiqh. To neutralize the risk of LLM hallucination, the system employs a Grammar Guided Constrained Decoding mechanism. We formally define the mapping function that dynamically constructs unambiguous right linear grammars from the FSA execution history. The paper mathematically proves that this architecture, augmented by a Bounded Neutral Lexicon and Valence Locking, guarantees the absolute absence of Decisional Hallucination, Juristic Factual Hallucination, and Semantic Inversion reducing the LLM to a pure linguistic transformer of deterministic juristic facts

</details>


### [110] [Ballad: Bandit-Based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17241)

Abstract: Modern automated heuristic discovery (AHD) systems increasingly rival hand-crafted heuristics by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to iteratively mutate and refine solutions. A natural assumption is that more powerful and more expensive models consistently yield better heuristics. We challenge this empirically: across multiple NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, we find no consistent advantage for larger, costlier models. This raises a deeper question: given a pool of LLMs spanning frontier and freely hosted or open-weight models, how should one adaptively select which model to invoke at each step of the search? The challenge is non-trivial. The optimal LLM may shift over the course of a run, and because one LLM's output seeds the context for subsequent calls, each model's reward signal is non-stationary and entangled across the heuristic population, thus violating the independence assumptions of classical online decision-making settings. We address this through Ballad (Bandit-based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery), an online bandit algorithm that learns to orchestrate a pool of LLMs into an ensemble that matches or exceeds what any soloist LLM achieves alone. This orchestration is powered by an LLM selection policy that favors rare, high-scoring heuristics over stable averages, a DAG-based credit propagation mechanism through the genealogy of the heuristic population, and discounting of stale observations to remain attuned to each model's current utility. Across five NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems and a pool of four models of varying capability and cost, Ballad not only matches the best individual LLM in hindsight but, in several cases, surpasses every individual model while reducing API cost by up to 65.6% relative to always using the strongest model.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce GeoWorld, a controllable and continuously evolving geographic environment platform for LLM agents, and LiveGeo, a benchmark for evaluating reliable decision-making in real-world geographic environments using GeoWorld. Unlike existing evaluations that largely reason and plan over fixed geographic states, GeoWorld is grounded in real-world geographic structure and allows external world states to evolve along their own temporal processes while preserving verifiable state histories. LiveGeo places geographic tasks within this evolving environment, requiring agents to actively acquire task-relevant information, verify whether the evidence supporting prior decisions remains valid, and revise their plans when necessary. Our results reveal that even frontier LLMs struggle with static tasks, with no model consistently excelling across tasks, while strong static performance does not ensure dynamic reliability. Further analysis identifies a key bottleneck: agents struggle to acquire and translate relevant evidence into valid decisions and maintain their correctness as the world evolves. By releasing LiveGeo together with its GeoWorld platform, we aim to provide the community with a flexible and extensible infrastructure for studying the next generation of agents capable of reliable action in evolving geographic worlds.

</details>

Abstract: Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.

</details>

Abstract: Applying machine learning systems to new domains introduces bottlenecks in both labeled data acquisition and manual pipeline engineering. While recent MLE agents show promise in automating pipeline design, they have been studied almost exclusively in fully supervised settings, leaving them untested in realistic regimes where labeled data is scarce but unlabeled data is abundant. In this work, we introduce Agentic Semi-Supervised Learning (AgentSSL), framing SSL as a program search problem and studying how agents navigate this space under limited supervision. We find that even under weak feedback and matched compute, agents can synthesize code that combines and adapts established SSL techniques, exceeding the strongest human-engineered baselines in 8 of 12 settings by up to +21.8%. However, our results identify program selection as a critical bottleneck in AgentSSL, where performance drops by as much as 47% when selecting based on the agent's feedback metric. To systematically study this bottleneck, we introduce ProgramSelectBench, a large-scale benchmark with a combined total of 16,977 agent-generated and human-engineered SSL programs, and evaluate both unsupervised and active program selection methods. We further evaluate AgentSSL on real-world ecology detection tasks, highlighting the opportunities and challenges of transferring agent-discovered strategies beyond controlled benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Respiratory and bulk motion displace the spinal cord between the volumes of a functional MRI time series. Current methods such as the Spinal Cord Toolbox (SCT) take several minutes per time series, and general-purpose brain tools register whole volumes, whereas the cord moves mainly slice by slice. We propose a lightweight convolutional neural network that regresses the in-plane translation aligning each axial slice to a reference. Two models, trained without motion labels, correct large and then residual motion. On a 3T (120 scans) and a 7T (59 scans) dataset, the proposed method improves on SCT on every metric (tSNR +10 to +25%, DVARS -20 to -38%), leaves almost no slice displaced by more than one voxel (<0.1%), performs on par with a slice-wise AFNI variant and better than slice-wise FSL FLIRT, and runs in about 10 s per scan, about 30 times faster than SCT. The code is available at https://github.com/ivadomed/moco-dl and the method is available in SCT v7.4.

</details>


### [115] [What Entropy-Based View Selection Cannot See: An Audit of Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14686)
*Sivasuthan Sukumar,Uthayasanker Thayasivam*

Main category: cs.CV

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Vision-language models such as CLIP can classify images into categories they were never explicitly trained to recognize, but accuracy degrades when test images differ in style or source from their training data. A common remedy generates randomly cropped and flipped copies of each test image, called views, and combines their predictions. Since some views miss the object, methods must identify which views to trust. The dominant approach uses prediction entropy to measure confidence and retains the most certain views. Other methods use signals such as embedding typicality. Despite repeated refinements of these scoring functions, the information they rely on, the model's own response to each view, remains largely unexamined. We investigate this choice while holding other pipeline stages fixed. First, we show that view selection, rather than the gradient update, accounts for almost all accuracy gains. We then examine whether entropy identifies useful views when predictions are wrong, despite at least one view predicting the correct class. In these cases, entropy ranks correct views above incorrect ones no better than chance, recovering only about one-twentieth of the accuracy recovered by an oracle over the same views. This finding holds across two image encoders and under a control ruling out an obvious statistical artifact. An exact analysis explains this limitation: entropy measures confidence but not which class a view favors. We identify an unused signal already available in the augmentation pipeline: the fraction of the original image retained by each crop. Ranking views by this quantity improves accuracy on 14 of 15 benchmark datasets over entropy and on 11 of 15 over the strongest scoring function tested. A pre-method measurement also predicts which datasets benefit.

</details>


### [116] [FreeDeshadow: Shadow Removal for User-Specified Objects With Multimodal Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14608)

Abstract: Existing shadow removal methods either perform global shadow removal or rely on precise shadow masks to guide restoration. Such paradigms limit their ability to selectively remove the shadow cast by a user-specified object in practical applications. To this end, we introduce an object-aware controllable shadow removal task, which allows users to specify a target object through a simple prompt and removes only its cast shadow while preserving other content. However, object-shadow asymmetry makes it challenging to establish accurate correspondence between the specified object and its spatially separated cast shadow and to confine restoration to the target shadow region. To address these challenges, we propose FreeDeshadow, a unified controllable shadow removal framework that supports both visual and textual prompts. It consists of an object-aware generator and a shadow-aware local refiner. The former interprets the user prompt and produces a high-quality shadow-free proposal, while the latter establishes the correspondence between the object and its cast shadow and constrains the generated changes to the target shadow region. To support training and evaluation, we further construct PCSRD+, a large-scale dataset containing 154,571 quadruples at 1024$\times$1024 resolution and covering six types of user prompts. The visual prompts include a dot, a line, and an object mask, while the textual prompts describe the target object using attributes, spatial relations, or states. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FreeDeshadow accurately removes the shadow of the target object under both visual and textual prompts while effectively preserving non-target regions. It also outperforms existing methods in shadow removal performance and controllability. Project Page: https://wzlwlm13.github.io/FreeDeshadow

</details>

Abstract: Englacial layers in radio-echo-sounding radargrams (subsurface ice images) encode the climate and flow history of ice sheets and glaciers. However, large-scale extraction is hindered by time-consuming manual annotation and semi-automated detectors that produce fragmented rather than continuous horizons. We address two challenges: reconstructing complete layers from partial detections across radargram profiles and validating layer continuity across adjacent tile boundaries. We introduce a slope-aware greedy fragment-linking algorithm that reconstructs full-width layers using row-similarity gating and an exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) slope predictor. Each reconstruction receives a content-based hash ID for stable cross-radargram identification. For quantitative evaluation, we generate SyntheIce, a synthetic dataset of 600 radargrams spanning multiple complexity levels and realistic noise conditions, including 150 wide radargrams divided into four adjacent tiles. All samples include exact ground-truth annotations. We evaluate the dataset using XCheck, a consistency-based validation framework, extending its original two-image setting to four consecutive tiles and enforcing continuity at every boundary. This demonstrates the dataset's utility for developing and evaluating real-world annotation algorithms. Our findings extend beyond ice science: when spatial features are fragmented but retain consistent local geometry, trajectory-based linking is more reliable than appearance-based matching. Evaluation against expert-labeled real radargrams demonstrates high precision, recall, F1-score, and mean IoU, supporting the method's transfer to real-world data.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-camera rigs ship pre-calibrated from the manufacturer, but their calibration goes stale each time the rig is repositioned, and drifts continuously through wear, thermal cycling, and vibration. Recovering it on-the-fly is hard: classical bundle adjustment needs a fresh multi-view capture and a global optimization whose cost grows with the rig, ruling out real-time use. In this paper we present RECALL, a feed-forward method that recalibrates a movable multi-camera rig in a single pass. From the nominal OEM calibration as an anchor and a brief observation of a known object, it predicts each camera's current intrinsics and extrinsics, written as a shared global rotation of the rig composed with a small per-camera drift. Instead of regressing the calibration in one shot, RECALL refines its estimate with a learned, rig-coupled recurrent operator that reprojects through a differentiable projection and applies attention across the cameras at each step, and solves for the shared rotational gauge in-network with a learned Gauss-Newton step rather than regressing it. Because the ground truth is synthesizable, we train entirely on data generated on-the-fly and need no captured dataset. On a synthetic 10-camera benchmark, RECALL is more accurate than per-camera and rig-coupled joint bundle adjustment ($0.26^\circ$ vs $0.47^\circ$ rotation, $5.4$ vs $8.9$ px focal) and runs about $10^5\times$ faster at about $0.12$ ms per rig. On real data (TotalCapture, CMU Panoptic) it matches or beats offline rig-coupled bundle adjustment on absolute and relative rotation while running four-to-five orders of magnitude faster, and on the Panoptic surround rig it also matches the strongest image-only foundation models (VGGT, VGGT-$Ω$, $π^3$) on relative pose while staying metric and about $15\times$ more accurate in focal length. Code: https://github.com/theICTlab/RECALL-multicam

</details>

Abstract: Driving world models aim to simulate how a scene unfolds under different ego actions. This requires plausible motion of surrounding vehicles as well as precise ego-trajectory control. Learning vehicle motion from perspective video alone is challenging because motion is entangled with appearance and vehicles can be occluded. Prior work therefore conditions video generation on layouts that specify vehicle motion, providing control over surrounding vehicles but requiring their future trajectories as inputs. We introduce BirdDreams, an autoregressive driving world model that jointly generates perspective video and a schematic bird's-eye-view (BEV) video, making scene layout part of the predicted future. The BEV, rendered from scene annotations for training, depicts roads and vehicles as simple shapes at a fixed metric scale, providing a top-down view of their positions and motion with reduced occlusion between vehicles. Perspective video, by contrast, retains scene details that the BEV omits, such as traffic signals and object appearance. Joint attention between two video diffusion transformers, one per stream, lets each stream inform the other during generation. The predicted BEV also provides a geometric interface for ego control: we translate and rotate its latest frame along an input ego trajectory and use the resulting frames as a soft condition that shows how the scene would appear to move if only the ego vehicle moved. At inference, BirdDreams generates both videos from a single camera image without requiring an HD map or vehicle-state inputs. On PhysicalAI-AV and nuScenes, BirdDreams follows input ego trajectories more faithfully than prior driving world models and generates more plausible ego motion without ego-action conditioning. Human evaluation shows that BirdDreams outperforms a perspective-only baseline in generating realistic surrounding-vehicle behavior.

</details>

Abstract: Cataloguing a Roman Imperial coin involves reading its legends and identifying its imagery in order to match it to an entry in the standard reference catalogue. Previous work has automated individual parts of this process, such as identifying the issuer or recognizing reverse motifs, by formulating each part as a classification task over a predefined set of labels. We instead formulate coin description as image-to-text generation and adapt an open-weight vision-language model (VLM), Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, to four tasks: transcribing the obverse and reverse legends, and describing the obverse and reverse imagery. To obtain training data, we convert approximately 30,000 auction records into structured image-text pairs. We separate the two sides of each coin, extract and normalize legends and descriptions, and standardize obverse descriptions against a bust-type taxonomy. We then train a separate low-rank adapter for each task on a single 20GB GPU, and evaluate each task with metrics that we define to capture the key components of numismatic descriptions. The adapted models outperform both the unadapted base model and GPT-4.1 on all four tasks, reducing the normalized edit distance of legend transcriptions by over 40% relative to GPT-4.1. The improvement is smaller, but still present, on legends absent from the training data.

</details>

Abstract: As particle-based simulations grow in scale, efficient compression of scientific data becomes critical. Neural point cloud compression (PCC) methods excel on computer vision (CV) benchmarks, but do they transfer to scientific particle data? We highlight that scientific particles differ from CV point clouds in fractal dimension: CV point clouds are locally planar, but particles are not. This key difference limits the effectiveness of the octree data structure that underpins most PCC methods. Neural codecs achieve only marginal bitrate reductions over the traditional G-PCC baseline on scientific data, revealing a significant performance gap. Supported by an evaluation spanning domains of cosmology, molecular dynamics, and plasma physics, we outline where the structure of scientific data limits current neural approaches and where learning can help in the future.

</details>

Abstract: Open-weight Vision-Language Models (VLMs) score 85-94% on spatial benchmarks (BLINK Spatial, What's Up CLEVR) yet collapse to chance on simple geometric questions when object, scene, and texture priors are stripped away. We introduce and open-source Sophon, a procedurally generated diagnostic of paired colored point-cloud stimuli across three coordinate systems (1D linear, 2D Cartesian, 2D polar) and four question types (binary, categorical, comparative, quantitative). Across four open architectures (LLaVA-NeXT, LLaVA-OneVision, Qwen2.5-VL, Idefics3), accuracy clusters at 47-52% while frontier models (Opus 4.6, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-5.4) clear 77-83% and humans reach 84-94%. We use linear probes and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to trace the open-weight failures. Probes of the vision encoder (ViT) and language decoder (LLM) recover Sophon spatial information at 83-85% and 80-86% accuracy, respectively, and the language tower's reasoning circuit answers text-only controls (point clouds as sets of coordinates) at +40 pp over visual stimuli. Language tower per-layer attention attribution localizes visual integration to mid-stack layers. At readout, models elevate the correct answer-set tokens but select among them randomly. We localize the mechanism by tuning individual and grouped components across every architecture's stack, including the vision tower (ViT workspace and reader), the projector, and the language tower (LLM workspace and reader). These experiments demonstrate that this mid-stack workspace holds circuitry capable of organizing the spatial signal so that the frozen reader can decode it. LoRA fine-tuning adjusts that circuitry such that 7B-class VLMs answer Sophon spatial reasoning questions at frontier aggregate accuracy. This work suggests that these models do not have a capability gap for spatial reasoning but suffer from a routing bottleneck that targeted fine-tuning repairs.

</details>

Abstract: We present OrthoPairGTL, a geometric training framework for cross-domain deepfake detection, built on a partially frozen DINOv3 backbone ($\sim$300M parameters), and make three contributions: (1) We re-evaluate six recent transfer-learning detectors (Forensics Adapter, Effort, three GenD variants and FakeSTormer) using the authors' released checkpoints under a single preprocessing pipeline across 17 cross-domain datasets (2018-2025), so that the reported gaps reflect method rather than per-method pipeline tuning. (3) Under LoRA, this regularisation gives a more stable optimisation profile: GSD-LoRA's post-peak validation degradation is $4.2\times$ shallower than plain LoRA at the same parameter footprint, and the time-averaged validation AUC across training is 1.82 pp higher. On the 17 datasets, GSD-LoRA matches or exceeds every re-evaluated baseline (92.82% vs. 91.58% for the strongest; paired Wilcoxon signed-rank $p=0.015$, 13/17 per-dataset wins), with the largest gains on modern reenactment-heavy benchmarks (DSv1.1, DSv2). Under strict parameter budgets, LayerNorm-only tuning ($\approx$0.03% of the backbone) is the most stable configuration and matches the strongest baseline with a mean of 91.97%.

### [124] [Honest Fusion Evaluation for Cross-Domain Deepfake Detection: Leakage Audits and Tail Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14856)
*Luke Collins,Chang-Tsun Li,Duc Thanh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Cross-domain video deepfake detection increasingly uses frozen foundation-model experts, combined by calibrated score averaging, a per-video router picking one expert, or a learned head over concatenated features. Expressive routing and feature fusion are often reported to beat plain averaging. Across a 22-dataset frozen-expert testbed, we find those gains are largely evaluation artifacts. A corner theorem locates the gap routing aims to close: because AUC depends only on score ordering, the best any score-space router can do is an oracle reading the test label to pick each video's expert, a ceiling no realisable, label-blind router reaches. The theorem does not order real routers against a calibrated global average; the case against routing is empirical, and no router we test beats that average. Feature fusion reads raw features, falls outside the theorem, and is audited empirically. Reported gains trace to leakage: holding out one dataset leaks siblings sharing identities and reals (over-crediting routing), while holding out one manipulation keeps that dataset's reals in training (over-crediting feature fusion). A $2\times 2$ over fold and evaluation granularity pins the $+2.6$pp feature-fusion "win" to the fold alone: 12/12 significant under leaky folds vs. 0/12 under honest family-held-out folds, where both gains vanish. Fusion still pays off in the tail of hard videos and families: as experts are added, videos every expert misclassifies drop from 15.2% to 2.3%, and global calibrated fusion (InvB) beats the best single expert on worst-family AUC, selective risk (AURC), and low-false-positive recall, stably across protocols. Practitioners should calibrate and combine globally, hold out whole dataset families, and measure tail performance. For score-space routing under ranking metrics, honest evaluation and expert diversity matter more than combiner complexity.

</details>

Abstract: This article presents the successful in-orbit validation of an object-based change detection pipeline for low latency maritime monitoring onboard the CogniSAT-6 mission. We propose and validate a lightweight, three-phase operational sequence: optimized ground-based dual-acquisition scheduling, real-time onboard AI-based vessel segmentation, and multi-temporal matching. Upon acquiring consecutive images over a targeted Area of Interest (AoI), the onboard system correlates vessel detections using a hybrid matching approach that integrates the Hungarian algorithm for optimal assignment with RANSAC-driven spatial and size filters for outlier rejection. A series of in-orbit validations demonstrated robust performance across 11 diverse maritime scenarios, achieving valid recall and precision values (considering only vessel size and location) exceeding 0.8. The validation demonstrated the ability of the system to track both cooperative targets and uncooperative "dark" vessels strictly through geometric consistency. This capacity to autonomously generate and act upon real-time intelligence establishes a foundation for next-generation Insight-as-a-Service (IaaS) and tipping-and-cueing architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Inertial odometry (IO) using only Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) enables camera-free head tracking in head-mounted devices such as VR headsets, AR glasses, and earbuds. Prior IO methods operate in a gravity-aligned world frame, which is not yaw-equivariant. Yaw augmentation improves robustness but does not guarantee equivariance; learned equivariant layers achieve it, but EqNIO's full pipeline costs nearly 3$\times$ the FLOPs of ours. While raw body-frame IMU inputs are inherently yaw-invariant, head tilt redistributes both gravity and motion signals across device axes, making the representation tilt-variant. We propose HeadIO, which combines a body-frame heading decomposition with a bidirectional Mamba backbone for head-mounted inertial odometry. Our decomposition achieves tilt invariance by separating gravity-aligned and horizontal components using only the tilt component of device orientation. We evaluate on four head-mounted datasets and find that HeadIO improves RTE-5s by 8.1% (0.260 m $\to$ 0.239 m) over world-frame methods with learned equivariant layers on Nymeria, the largest benchmark dataset, at nearly 3$\times$ lower FLOPs and 8$\times$ fewer parameters. HeadIO achieves state-of-the-art performance on our primary metric, RTE-5s, on all Aria-glasses datasets, which natively provide body-frame semantics for our decomposition method. We further evaluate the IMU-only deployment scenario on Nymeria, where HeadIO remains state-of-the-art. We show that our method relies mainly on tilt accuracy, which can be obtained from the gravity signal; for yaw, a single-coefficient gyroscope correction removes 11.6% of the yaw error and improves RTE-5s by 2.5%, and sparse external yaw corrections (for example, one every 30 s) can improve RTE-5s by 12.0%. Our decomposition is lightweight, composes with any backbone, and is suitable for real-time deployment on head-mounted devices.

</details>

Abstract: AI-generated content (AIGC) has substantially narrowed the visual gap between natural and synthetic images, while existing detectors often rely on generator-specific artifacts and exhibit limited generalization to unseen models. This motivates a fundamental question: which intrinsic physical image characteristics provide stable, discriminative cues for distinguishing natural from AI-generated images? We investigate this by systematically identifying robust physical features and incorporating them into a vision-language representation without external textual prompts at inference. We first analyze 15 handcrafted physical descriptors across images from over 20 generative models. Based on cross-generator stability and real-fake discriminability, we propose a Feature Stability-Discriminability Validation Assessment (FSDVA) to select a compact set of reliable features. Five core features, including Laplacian variance, Sobel statistics, and residual-noise variance, are then used to construct structured textual descriptions encoding physical evidence for CLIP-based learning. We integrate these physics-enriched descriptions with image representations via a native physics-guided multimodal framework, adapting CLIP ViT-L 14 with LoRA. At inference, the model requires only the input image, with no external captions or manual prompts. On GenImage, our method achieves 99.0 percent accuracy on SDv1.4 and 96.7 percent on Wukong, with a mean of 97.23 percent across eight generative models versus 95.8 percent for C2pCLIP under the same setting. On UniversalFakeDetect, it maintains strong, stable generalization across diverse and unseen generation settings. These results show that carefully selected physical image statistics provide complementary forensic evidence to pretrained vision-language representations, enabling interpretable, more generator-agnostic AIGC detection.

</details>

Abstract: Assessing human creativity from drawings provides a scalable way to evaluate human creative ability. However, existing approaches either rely on costly expert annotation or treat creativity as a single regression target, overlooking its underlying structure. Psychological theories have long emphasized that creativity emerges from balancing novelty and appropriateness. Motivated by this principle, we recast creativity as structured semantic deviation between paired drawings, where novelty measures the extent of departure from the original base sketch, and appropriateness determines whether such departures remain meaningful. This formulation naturally leads to a hyperbolic representation, in which novelty is quantified by geodesic distance and appropriateness is evaluated conditionally via a learned decision boundary, further regulated by an abstraction-conditioned adaptive constraint. Experiments on AuDrA-Drawings demonstrate state-of-the-art alignment with expert judgments and strong robustness under rater and task shifts. Beyond performance gains, our framework reveals an interpretable radial organization of creative variation, offering a principled geometric foundation for automatic creativity assessment.

</details>

Abstract: Reasoning segmentation requires identifying the target referred to by a query among visually similar objects, yet trajectory-level rewards cannot directly indicate which tokens provide relevant visual evidence. We propose Hint-Informed Self-Distillation (HISD), which uses target-disambiguation evidence for token-level credit assignment. An external model generates Segment Hints offline from training images, queries, and ground-truth target annotations, organizing target identity, discriminative attributes, confusable objects, and anchoring paths. A hint-conditioned exponential moving average teacher scores the student's own trajectories, and HISD computes the teacher-to-student evidence ratio over a token support set to adjust only the magnitudes of positive group-relative advantages, leaving nonpositive advantages unchanged. This design encourages concise reasoning while retaining the information needed for target disambiguation, and the student requires neither hints nor a teacher at inference. Under a unified training protocol, HISD improves ReasonSeg test gIoU and cIoU over our reproduction of DPAD by 3.00 and 3.55 percentage points, respectively. Across the five evaluated splits of ReasonSeg and the RefCOCO family, HISD achieves higher segmentation metrics than this baseline and reduces the average token count of valid reasoning spans by 80.17 to 91.83 percent.

</details>

Abstract: Reconstructing video from degraded measurements requires priors that capture both frame appearance and temporal dynamics. Many recent approaches combine pretrained image diffusion priors with motion guidance, sidestepping the costs of training large video models. Temporal consistency is often enforced via photometric losses or warping-based constraints, which implicitly assume that motion explains frame-to-frame or latent-to-latent changes up to small deviations, an assumption that fails under occlusions, lighting changes, and non-rigid deformation. In this paper, we propose to learn the coupling between motion and frame appearance as a generative prior. We learn the distribution of motion-induced appearance residuals with a conditional diffusion model and use it to regularize reconstruction in video inverse problems. Our motion-appearance prior acts as a plug-in regularizer for diffusion-based video reconstruction, preserving the benefits of image diffusion models while remaining compatible with existing appearance regularization techniques. Experiments on dynamic video inverse problems show that reconstruction with a learnt motion-appearance coupling prior outperforms photometric-consistency and noise-warping baselines, and that the approach also transfers to cardiac MRI reconstruction.

</details>

Abstract: AI-manipulated video detectors are typically evaluated on clean, curated datasets. In deployment, however, videos have usually passed through repost compression on social-media platforms and sometimes were captured by imperfect hardware. To measure detection under these conditions, we introduce Reality Perception Gap (RPG), a benchmark of 10,000 videos from frontier generators and partially synthetic edits, processed with cross-platform repost compression and paired with real footage that carries camera-side optical artifacts. RPG also includes socially fragile content, such as crime and wartime footage, where fabricated videos can cause severe social harm. Across 18 detectors, none catches more than 15% of fakes at a 1% false-positive rate. A positive control and a re-encoding sweep separate two sources of failure: generator and content shift degrades detectors before any compression, and repost compression erases the low-level traces that the artifact-based detectors relies on. Existing detectors therefore struggle when deployment conditions strip away the generation artifacts they rely on. These complementary failures suggest that robust detection requires training under deployment conditions and combining low-level, temporal, and semantic evidence, where passive detection may have reached its limits.

</details>

Abstract: Volumetric CT super-resolution (SR) models are commonly trained on synthetic or physically acquired pairs of low-resolution (LR) and high-resolution (HR) volumes. However, synthetic pairs miss real acquisition effects, whereas registered physical pairs retain inter-scan noise, artifact, and microstructure differences---causing direct voxelwise regression, and even naive synthetic-real mixing, to oversmooth fine porous structures. We introduce the Physically-anchored Implicit Network (PIN) with Real-Pair Dual-Anchor learning, which decouples physical-pair supervision into two complementary roles. The HR anchor teaches fine structure from registered HR through pair-conditioned spectral replay, while the LR anchor constrains the prediction from acquired LR via affine-calibrated low-pass projection. Together with exact synthetic supervision in one shared volumetric implicit model, PIN learns from both physical scans without forcing the acquired-LR reconstruction to match registered HR voxel by voxel. Inference requires only acquired LR. Across 30 external baseline configurations on real CT, PIN achieves the lowest full-volume LPIPS, reduces average symmetric surface distance by 42.3-49.5% and bone-volume-fraction error by 52.2-53.4% relative to the three strongest baselines for each metric, and lowers the erased reference-pore volume fraction from 37.3-72.9% to 10.4%. Code, pretrained models, and evaluation scripts will be publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Aerial spatial intelligence (ASI) requires models to understand objects and spatial relationships across changing viewpoints and scales. Evaluating these abilities as models evolve calls for fresh physical observations, yet producing new aerial spatial questions requires repeatedly establishing entity identities, geometric references, and cross-view correspondences. We introduce \textbf{RSLiveBench-UAV}, a continually evolving vision-language benchmark driven by recurring acquisition of real UAV imagery. Its update protocol admits images captured after the preceding release and delays answer disclosure. An entity-centered production workflow uses geometry-assisted association to link repeated observations of physical vehicles, reuse human-verified attributes, and combine them with view-specific annotations and spatial references for traceable question generation. All tasks use deterministic scoring without model judges. We evaluate 30 general-purpose, remote-sensing, and embodied models on 35,491 question records from ten scenes, covering 17 tasks across 2D perception, 3D perception, cross-view perception, and reasoning. All 18 tested embodied models outperform the three selected remote-sensing comparators in Overall, although the strongest embodied model scores 43.29 versus 59.88 for GPT-6-Astra. Fine-grained vehicle recognition remains challenging, and performance varies across heights and camera pitches. Retrospective scene-group comparisons and rolling-window replay characterize score-pattern consistency under scene renewal. RSLiveBench-UAV connects fresh physical observations, entity-centered answer production, and systematic model evaluation to support continual assessment of ASI. Benchmark will release at https://rslivebench.space

</details>

Abstract: Ultrasound elastography (or elasticity imaging) is a method that characterizes the tissue mechanical properties with the purpose of aiding the diagnosis of certain pathologies. Many novel clinical applications use the assumption that these tissue properties are changed in the presence of pathological processes. Characterization of tissue mechanical properties is achieved by imaging the response of the tissue to an induced stress or excitation. A plethora of approaches have been used in the scientific community to induce excitation in tissue, which usually require additional tools or devices than are costly. Furthermore these are usually integrated in the imaging pipeline using additional software or hardware for control. An alternative that is simpler, faster to integrate (and low-cost) is proposed in this work, by using readily available smartphones as a source of mechanical excitation. The induced displacements are used to solve the elasticity inverse problem (IP) using a finite element method (FEM) approach. Preliminary elasticity reconstructions of a phantom that mimics tissue are similar to when using a dedicated actuator as means of inducing harmonic displacements, illustrating the feasibility of our low-cost ultrasound elasography approach.

</details>

Abstract: Hydration influences physiology and daily function, yet repeated practical measurement in everyday settings remains limited. We present initial real-world evidence that a facial-video lifestyle digital biomarker score can track the direction of hydration state transitions. Using Ramadan fasting as a model of prolonged fluid abstinence, we assessed whether the score tracks the transition between the fasting (pre-drink) state and the rehydrated (post-drink) state across three validation layers. In the primary cohort (104 participants, 208 videos), the score increased from a mean of 36.6 in the fasting (pre-drink) state to 70.6 in the rehydrated (post-drink) state (change = +33.9, 95% CI [+30.7, +37.1], p < 0.001, Cohen's d = 2.03), with 102 of 104 participants (98.1%) showing an upward shift. In a nested body-weight subgroup (20 participants), body weight increased by +0.74 kg after fluid intake (p < 0.001), confirming fluid uptake and providing supportive physical anchoring. In a nested serum osmolality subgroup (10 participants), osmolality decreased by -5.1 mOsm/kg (p < 0.001), with all post-drink values within the normal clinical range, providing exploratory biological support. An exploratory secondary comparison of water versus electrolyte rehydration showed no statistically significant difference in short-term score change (p = 0.247). This study does not establish a clinical diagnostic test for dehydration. Rather, these findings support the score as a lifestyle digital biomarker for tracking the direction of hydration state change in real-world settings.

</details>

Abstract: Low-light image and video enhancement has received increasing attention in recent years. However, most existing enhancement methods are developed and evaluated on still images, while progress in low-light video enhancement remains limited by the lack of real-world paired video datasets. Acquiring paired low-light and normal-light video sequences is challenging because it requires spatially and temporally aligned captures while avoiding motion blur, exposure mismatch, and correspondence errors in dynamic scenes. To address this gap, we introduce PiLLVD, the Pi-based Low-Light Video Dataset, a paired low-light video dataset captured using an accessible Raspberry Pi-based multi-camera system with off-the-shelf cameras. PiLLVD provides paired low-light and aligned normal-light reference sequences for dynamic indoor and outdoor scenes, including diverse human and object motion under varying illumination conditions. Unlike existing datasets that are often image-based, static, synthetic, or limited to simple motion patterns, PiLLVD focuses on motion-rich scenes where frame-to-frame stability and motion-related artefacts are important for evaluation. In addition to presenting the dataset and acquisition pipeline, we benchmark representative pre-trained low-light video enhancement methods on PiLLVD under a zero-shot setting. The results show that existing methods do not generalise uniformly across the proposed sequences, highlighting the challenges posed by the proposed dataset and its value for both benchmarking existing methods and supporting the development of future low-light video enhancement algorithms.

</details>

Abstract: Federated learning often assumes that data is fixed in advance, which is unrealistic in many real-world scenarios where new data continuously arrives, causing catastrophic forgetting. To address this challenge, we propose FCLPF (Federated Class Incremental Learning with Pseudo Features), a method that uses pseudo features generated from prototypes to mitigate catastrophic forgetting. Our approach reduces communication costs and improves efficiency by eliminating the need for past data and avoiding computationally heavy models like GANs. Experimental results on CIFAR-100 show that FCLPF achieves an average accuracy of 51.87% and an average forgetting of 9.62%, significantly outperforming existing baselines with an average accuracy of 47.72% and forgetting of 20.46%. On TinyImageNet, FCLPF achieves 37.56% accuracy and 3.14% forgetting, compared to the baselines' 27.69% accuracy and 24.46% forgetting, demonstrating the superior performance of FCLPF.

</details>

Abstract: Face presentation attack detection requires representations that distinguish physical spoofing cues across changes in acquisition devices, subjects, and attack media. Large visual encoders offer transferable representations, but conventional binary fine-tuning supervises their final decisions without explicitly constraining how local facial evidence is represented under partial observation. We present CP-JEPA, a context-predictive adaptation framework that combines low-rank tuning of a pretrained CLIP image encoder with a joint-embedding predictive objective. A student predicts complete-image teacher features at masked spatial locations, while unmasked classification, masked classification, and prediction consistency retain the connection to the live/spoof task. An exponential-moving-average teacher supplies evolving latent targets. The auxiliary components are used only during training; inference requires only the LoRA-adapted CLIP visual encoder and binary classifier. {We evaluate CP-JEPA under single-source and multi-source OCIM transfer, additional CelebA-Spoof training, transfer to CelebA-Spoof, two-source training, and unseen-attack evaluation on SiW-Mv2.} On the standard OCIM benchmark, CP-JEPA achieves 3.74\% mean HTER and 98.73\% mean AUC. {Single-source evaluation achieves 11.36\% mean HTER across twelve source--target combinations.} With CelebA-Spoof added to the three OCIM training sources, CP-JEPA reduces mean HTER from 3.45\% to 2.35\% and increases mean AUC from 98.68\% to 99.32\%. On SiW-Mv2, it achieves 3.36\% mean HTER across the held-out attack categories. Controlled loss ablations examine the contributions of latent prediction, masked classification, and prediction consistency. The results show that context-predictive adaptation can improve cross-dataset PAD performance, although the gains vary with the training sources and evaluation setting.

</details>

Abstract: The visual world is cluttered: robust vision-language reasoning requires ignoring irrelevant information. Yet vision-language models (VLMs) can suffer substantial accuracy losses when answering questions about a target image with distractors present. Existing mitigations focus on selecting relevant images or limiting information exchange between images. Through a systematic analysis of three open 7-8B VLMs on five perception benchmarks adapted to include distractor images, we show that distractors reduce accuracy even when models correctly identify the relevant image, suggesting that selection alone is not enough. To understand the mechanism behind this interference, we combine attention analysis with targeted knockouts in Qwen2.5-VL and identify middle-layer distractor-to-text connections as a pathway through which irrelevant visual information affects reasoning. We introduce answer consistency regularization, which encourages consistent answer distributions with and without distractors. Without directly constraining attention, this objective reduces middle-layer attention from question tokens to distractor images, consistent with the pathway our analysis implicates. With five distractors, our approach improves circular accuracy by an average of 16 percentage points over the Qwen2.5-VL baseline across the five benchmarks while preserving single-image performance.

</details>

Abstract: Programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-L1) tumor proportion score (TPS) informs immunotherapy selection in non-small cell lung cancer. Near the 1% and 50% cutoffs, the score should also indicate when closer review is warranted. We designed a three-stage framework to follow the sequence of TPS assessment. Stage 1 identifies tumor regions and excludes macrophage-rich patches. Stage 2, a dual-branch cell counting network (D2Count-Net), counts PD-L1-positive and PD-L1-negative tumor cells in these regions to calculate TPS. Stage 3, TPS-Guard, an uncertainty-guided triage mechanism, reports model uncertainty as the disagreement among independently trained models, refers the most uncertain slides, and flags scores near the 1% or 50% cutoff when model estimates disagree across that cutoff. Flags state the reason for referral or alert the pathologist to possible scoring errors on auto-reported slides. In a retrospective, single-center study, 154 whole-slide images were scored by three pathologists, and the model and each pathologist were compared in turn with the mean of the other two. On the 114 slides unseen in training, model mean absolute error (MAE) was 6.96 percentage points, within the range of the three pathologists (4.24 to 8.29), and model uncertainty correlated with absolute TPS error (Spearman rho = 0.77) and inter-pathologist disagreement (rho = 0.71). After referral of the 15% most uncertain slides, model and pathologist MAE both decreased. Applied without retraining to an external, publicly available dataset, TPS-Guard still directed review to higher-error cases, and referring more uncertain cases raised sensitivity at the 50% cutoff. TPS-Guard provides reasons for reviewing individual automated scores and identifies slides that also produce greater disagreement among pathologists. Prospective reader studies should assess TPS-Guard's effect on TPS reporting.

</details>

Abstract: What makes an image creative? Although this may seem like a highly subjective decision, research in psychology suggests that creativity may be based on a set of fundamental creativity dimensions such as novelty, surprise, and arousal. A deeper understanding of image creativity can benefit both research and practical applications. Exploring how human viewers use creativity dimensions to evaluate image creativity may allow us to predict a target image's human-rated creativity automatically. We present a novel framework to estimate the perceived creativity of an image by: (1) predicting the strengths of an image's novelty, surprise, and arousal creativity dimensions, and (2) combining the creativity dimension strengths to estimate an image creativity score. Controlled human-subject experiments were conducted on Amazon Mechanical Turk to collect data to train supervised machine learning models to estimate an image's strengths on the three creativity dimensions. A final machine learning model takes the predicted dimension strengths as input to produce an estimated image creativity score. Results showed that our estimated creativity scores are statistically equivalent to human ratings of creativity for the images we tested. The novel contributions of our research are: (1) identification and validation of three psychological creativity dimensions used to estimate image creativity; (2) supervised machine learning models to automatically estimate novelty, surprise, and arousal strengths for an input image; (3) a supervised machine learning model to predict a creativity score from novelty, surprise, and arousal strengths for an input image, and (4) experimental methods to confirm the accuracy, advantages, and limitations of our approach.

</details>

Abstract: Quantifying disease-associated renal tubular remodeling is challenging because tubular appearances are heterogeneous. We introduce TuMAP (Tubular Morphotype-Aware Pathomics), a pipeline combining (1) dual-view foundation-model representations and provisional morphotype annotation, (2) compositional and within-morphotype pathomics, and (3) patient-level statistical inference. From 456,158 tubules in 358 PAS-stained kidney biopsy slides, TuMAP identified eight image-based morphotypes, seven of which showed stable subsampling assignments. Across 219 patients in six diagnostic groups, diabetic nephropathy (DN) and tubulointerstitial nephritis/acute tubular injury (TIN/ATI) each differed from minimal change disease in seven morphotype proportions, while IgA nephropathy differed in six. DN showed reduced nuclear solidity and increased eccentricity across all eight morphotypes. Area-normalized nuclear density exposed additional within-morphotype differences. TuMAP provides a framework for separating changes in tubular morphotype composition from phenotypic differences within morphotypes.

</details>

Abstract: Frozen vision-language models often match concepts mentioned in a caption without respecting their negation: a query "a kitchen with no toaster" favors an image containing a toaster. Yet these models are substantially stronger at matching affirmative descriptions. We exploit this asymmetry with Negation-Aware Evidence Scoring (NAES), which reformulates negated descriptions into affirmative queries and combines their evidence by caption structure. A lightweight text decomposer, NegSplit, identifies this structure by extracting negated spans and the remaining affirmative context. For captions with an affirmative remainder, NAES matches that content and applies a presence-based correction for the denial. When no affirmative content remains, it queries the denied object's presence and reflects the evidence around a reference to score the negated description. With LAION-2B CLIP, NAES achieves 82.4% accuracy on COCO-MCQ and 93.0% on VOC2007-MCQ, surpassing KTN by 2.9 and 0.9 percentage points; with OpenAI CLIP, it exceeds PeakPatch accuracy by 4.4 and 26.0 points, respectively. NAES also improves negated-description retrieval on COCO and MSR-VTT, with gains extending to SigLIP, SigLIP 2, and larger CLIP encoders. The strongest multiple-choice results use competing descriptions to center and align evidence; retrieval improvements persist without this conditioning. These findings show that explicitly separating affirmative matching from denied-object evidence can substantially improve negation-aware ranking.

</details>

Abstract: Cross-modal unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) object tracking faces modality-induced appearance discrepancies and abrupt target-position shifts that can arise from modality switching, UAV motion, and inter-sensor offsets. Although existing methods model modality and spatial variations, they primarily rely on offline representation learning and offer limited localization-oriented adaptation during testing. To address these challenges, we propose Test-Time Selective Adaptation with Token Shift (TSAT), which derives spatial and temporal adaptation signals from unlabeled test sequences for online optimization. Specifically, we design a controllable token shift auxiliary task, which imposes explicit spatial correspondence between predefined token translations and resultant localization offsets, generating stable annotation-free spatial adaptation signals. Furthermore, we introduce a temporal backward prediction auxiliary task to exploit frame-wise temporal dependencies, effectively mitigating cumulative localization errors in continuous tracking. To suppress interference from unreliable tracking samples, we develop a reliability-aware update gating strategy to screen valid adaptation signals and stabilize online parameter updates. Experiments on CM-UOT demonstrate that TSAT achieves state-of-the-art performance against existing test-time adaptation methods, exhibiting superior robustness under frequent modality switching and severe spatial displacement. Experiments across tracking frameworks and datasets further validate the effectiveness and generalization capability of TSAT.

</details>

Abstract: Visual token pruning reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models, but answer accuracy only partly reflects its effect. Pruning can change the probabilities of alternative response tokens even when greedy answers remain correct and unchanged. We compare full and pruned response distributions under shared reference prefixes. Average predictive drift grows with compression on fixed correctly answered samples. Separate analyses associate larger drift with more confidence-decision disagreements and sampling failures. We propose DiFiT (Distribution-Fidelity-guided Token pruning) to learn discrete selection from reference answers and full-token response distributions. Dual-View Distillation matches probabilities across the vocabulary, while answer supervision supports task quality. A learned scorer and differentiable Top-$K$ learn which visual evidence to retain. A multi-budget mask objective supports different retention ratios through one shared ranking over a frozen model. On Qwen2.5-VL-7B, DiFiT reduces sampling-induced negative flips by 30.3% relative to FastV at 10% retention on four-task greedy-correct controls. It preserves 98.74% of baseline performance on MME at 30% retention.

</details>


### [146] [SAVE: Less is More in Agentic Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15079)
*Tymoteusz Wilk,Sergey Prokudin,Xi Wang*

Abstract: Agentic video-reasoning frameworks iteratively explore a video, choosing what to observe based on the query and evidence gathered so far. Existing methods are typically evaluated with different backbones, preprocessing pipelines, and computational budgets, making it difficult to determine which components actually drive performance, how much they cost, and whether agentic inference is preferable to native video reasoning in the first place. We conduct a controlled evaluation of a diverse set of recent agentic video-reasoning systems across two strong contemporary backbones, with explicit token and monetary-cost accounting. Across Gemini 3 Flash and Gemma 4, most existing agentic frameworks fail to consistently improve over native video inference and often increase computational cost. We investigate two primary questions: (1) How much agentic framework complexity is necessary for effective video reasoning? (2) When, if at all, is agentic video reasoning preferable to just using the backbone directly? To answer the first question, we introduce a deliberately minimal Active Exploration Baseline and show that it remains competitive with substantially more elaborate agents. We then propose targeted extensions to this baseline, yielding SAVE (Simple Agentic Video Explorer). SAVE outperforms all evaluated prior agentic methods in five of six backbone-benchmark settings. Turning to the second question, our results indicate that the advantage of agentic exploration grows when dense processing would otherwise represent answer-relevant events too sparsely, particularly for long videos and temporally concentrated evidence. Overall, our results suggest that agentic video reasoning should favor simple frameworks over increasingly elaborate pipelines and focus primarily on long-form video, where selective evidence acquisition offers the clearest advantage over native inference.

</details>


### [147] [Prediction or Persistence? What Future-Prediction Scores Miss About World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15082)

Abstract: Pretrained video world models are compared and chosen as components for robot learning largely by future-prediction scores. On its own, such a score cannot be read as evidence of prediction, because it mixes encoder quality, the near future's resemblance to the recent past, and the predictor's own contribution. We separate the three with a matched control. On 27,000 future-retrieval trials from household egocentric video, a model must pick the true continuation of a clip among eleven candidates from the same video (chance 0.091), ranked zero-shot by its own training criterion. Each model is paired with a persistence control, an identical copy whose predictor is never invoked, so the paired difference isolates the predictor. Score and contribution dissociate within the native-video V-JEPA family alone: the predictor adds +0.291 top-1 for V-JEPA 2.1 but +0.03 to +0.04 for V-JEPA 2 on the identical trials. Across five models the contribution ranges from +0.291 to negative, and it is negative only for the two action-conditioned models, which we evaluate outside their training regime. Persistence alone recovers the encoder ordering expected from pretraining, a sign that the probe tracks real signal. We suggest reporting each future-prediction score together with its persistence baseline. The gap between them, not the score, is what makes the number interpretable: a necessary check costing one forward pass. Establishing downstream usefulness is a separate matter, for a planning or control evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Long-context multimodal intelligence requires revisiting earlier images as conversations and documents accumulate, yet retaining an image does not guarantee reliable access to its details. By varying context length, redistributing a fixed text passage around the image, and changing positions without adding text, we identify the positional relation between images and questions as a direct influence on visual access. To address this gap, we propose Visual Position Re-Anchoring (VPR), a training-free method that shifts cached visual-key rotary phases toward a short-context reference before question processing. It operates directly on the existing KV cache, preserving standard attention computation without extracting attention maps or adding model forward passes. We develop static and continuous compensation schedules, using the static schedule by default. Across ten checkpoints and ten benchmarks, static VPR improves 97 of 100 model--task scores. Further analysis shows greater attention to regions containing answer evidence across six checkpoints, with minimal runtime overhead under fixed rotary frequencies. These findings demonstrate an efficient way to sustain visual access through positional adjustment within the cache.

</details>

Abstract: Generating videos that faithfully convey intended semantics while remaining memorable to viewers is valuable for applications such as advertising, education, and storytelling. However, memorability-oriented text-to-video generation remains underexplored, as human memory involves complex neural processes that are difficult to model and translate into generation guidance. In this paper, we propose MemCrafter, brain-informed approach that enhances video memorability through iteratively agentic prompt refinement. The agentic framework contains two key components. First, we construct a cortical region-based neuro-memorability signal that predicts video memorability from estimated neural responses, providing both an optimization objective and interpretable region-level information. Second, we develop a sensitivity-aware feedback module, motivated by a conceptual sensitivity analysis of the optimization objective, to derive actionable guidance for prompt refinement while preserving semantic fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MemCrafter consistently improves predicted video memorability while maintaining semantic alignment and perceptual quality.

</details>

Abstract: Expert review of surgical videos is essential for surgical training but labor-intensive, motivating AI-assisted skill assessment to provide feedback at scale. Although surgical assessment rubrics may use different language to describe similar skills, existing automated methods commonly predict predefined skill levels or numerical scores tied to fixed rubrics, limiting their ability to transfer across semantically equivalent assessment criteria. We propose Predictive Rubric Alignment (PRA), a joint embedding prediction approach that scores surgical videos using natural-language descriptions of skill levels. Rather than matching level probabilities, PRA predicts a rater-weighted combination of skill-level description embeddings. Adjacent description features define a score-parameterized path, and the predicted embedding is projected onto this path to read the score. We introduce two variants: \textit{PRA-item}, which uses learned queries for predefined assessment items, and \textit{PRA-cond}, which generates queries from textual item names to enable assessment of unseen items without retraining. On AIxSuture, a dataset of expert-rated suturing videos, PRA outperforms published baselines in skill classification. We further evaluate zero-shot transfer to the smaller surgical skill assessment benchmark JIGSAWS. Trained on only two AIxSuture rubric items, PRA-cond assesses unseen JIGSAWS items without further training, yielding positive score correlations and greater Grad-CAM overlap with instruments and suture than a description-matching baseline. These results support predictive rubric alignment as a promising direction for flexible and explainable automated surgical skill assessment.

</details>


### [151] [Duo: A Reciprocal World Model for Full-Duplex Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15131)
*Hengyuan Cao,Biao Gong,Guoshan Lu,Qiyuan Zhang,Lin Liu,Yiding Tian,Yilei Jiang,Yuxin Ma,Chengzhang Wu,Kun Chen,Yiqian Xie,Ting Liu,Xin Zheng,Xijun Gu,Tianren Ma,Jiancheng Pan,Chi Zhang,Xuzhe Zheng,Siyi Li,Zhenzhong Lan*

Abstract: In this report, we introduce the Reciprocal World Model (RWM) and present Duo v1, a full-duplex world model that continuously perceives audiovisual inputs while responding to users in real time. The RWM treats intelligence and physical reality as equally fundamental to what constitutes a world. Existing world models largely focus on simulating the latter, leaving reasoning and decision-making as external controls rather than intrinsic parts of the world itself. In RWM, we identify two equally fundamental capabilities: Intent, which provides the cognition underlying world interactions, and Realization, which generates coherent audio-visual behavior. To realize this formulation, Duo v1 introduces three key technical designs: (1) Leveraging autoregressive modeling for continuous interaction reasoning and participation decisions, rather than restricting it to instruction understanding for diffusion models. (2) Constructing chunk-specific conditions and synchronize tokens across Intent and Realization on a unified timeline. Module-specific attention masks enforce causal dependencies both within and across chunks, preventing access to future inputs. (3) Mitigating distribution shift in both modules through dual rollout distillation, training on autoregressively generated Intent tokens and synthesized audiovisual histories from Realization. Both modules continuously perceive real-time audio, video, and text, enabling full-duplex interaction with spontaneous conversations and interruptions. Beyond conversation, users can share real objects, reshape virtual scenes, guide assistants, or play audiovisual games without mouse or keyboard controls. The release of Duo v1 makes our first step, with the next version coming soon. We look forward to broader exploration of this emerging field.

</details>

Abstract: Predictive video encoders provide dense visual representations, but adapting them to 3D reconstruction requires accurate depth, camera poses, and cross-view geometry. We study supervised RGB-only multi-view reconstruction using an architecture that combines video-pretrained features, camera estimation, scene aggregation, epipolar transport, and residual pointmaps. Experiments compare global and spatial camera regression with joint relative objectives on CO3D, alongside an independently trained TUM RGB-D model. On 168 CO3D five-view windows, the spatial-camera configuration achieves 29.3% lower absolute trajectory error than the global-camera configuration, but performs worse than identity poses in trajectory error and worse than the global configuration in depth error. The joint relative-objective configuration improves normalized intrinsics error and similarity-aligned Chamfer distance from 0.6447 to 0.5573, but increases raw Chamfer distance from 3.5535 to 4.4374. On 163 indoor windows, camera error is 0.3063 m versus 0.3694 m for identity poses, while depth error is 0.4279 versus 0.2516 for constant median depth. Refinement worsens indoor camera error, and additional experiments show competing depth and pose effects. Overall, the results distinguish representation adaptation, aligned shape, and raw reconstruction, highlighting the need for isolated component and encoder comparisons before attributing reconstruction gains to predictive video pretraining.

Abstract: High-dynamic-range (HDR) displays are now common, but most video content, such as archival footage, broadcast material, and the output of generative video models, exists only in standard dynamic range (SDR). The need is sharpest for generated video, which has no HDR master at all and is graded alongside live-action and visual-effects material finished in HDR. Converting SDR to HDR means undoing a tone-mapping operation that was never recorded, and clipping destroys the radiance wherever it acts, so HDR recovery is a blind inverse problem. Methods built on generative video priors fill clipped regions with plausible structure, but their reconstructions are systematically biased: in every public method we test, saturated content leans too bright or too dark, and the direction of the lean moves with the tone operator behind the input. We call this failure the luminance error, measure both its magnitude and its sign, and make it the focus of this work. Reframing ITM as posterior inference under an unknown operator locates the failure in two places and yields two corrections. A fixed affine functional of the video-diffusion latent reads log-luminance without decoding; on that coordinate an asymmetric loss selects an expectile of the posterior, applied only where clipping leaves luminance unconstrained. We also synthesize training SDR through an explicit family of tone operators, so the model marginalizes over what the observation cannot show. Trained this way, our luminance error stays flatter across operators and smaller on average than any public method, and the full system achieves the best perceptual quality on all three benchmarks.

</details>


### [154] [From Concepts to Interactions: Dynamic Graph Semantic Recomposition for Safe Visual Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15149)
*Qinghui Gong,Jinshan Lai,Xunlei Chen,Hua Meng,Zhengchun Zhou*
