Abstract: Many manipulation tasks require separating adhered, embedded, contaminated, or mechanically constrained targets. We study this setting as removal manipulation, where progress depends on evolving contact states that are only partially observable from vision and kinematics. We introduce DexRemo, a three-finger robotic hand with high-force actuation, modular fingertips, and three-axis fingertip force sensing, together with a nine-scenario real-robot task suite spanning adhesive detachment, surface cleaning and self-cleaning, and occluded retrieval. To make multi-finger force observations easier to fuse, we express fingertip forces in a shared wrist-centered frame, giving force components consistent geometric meaning across fingers and time. Using an Action Chunking Transformer backbone, policies fuse multi-view vision, proprioception, fingertip pose, and force observations to predict closed-loop action chunks from demonstrations. Evaluation uses six core benchmark tasks for controlled fingertip-input and force-frame ablations, plus three extension scenarios for additional held-out coverage. Across these evaluations, the learned policies achieve consistent removal performance, and ablations show that wrist-frame forces, especially with fingertip pose, improve stability and generalization over local-frame force inputs and no-force baselines. Project website: https://dexremo.github.io

Abstract: Industrial manipulation requires precise alignment and controlled handling of rigid, deformable, and flexible components. Yet, the benefits of pretrained visuomotor policies remain underexplored in settings where limited access to specialized hardware, components, and fixtures constrains demonstration collection and evaluation. Understanding task-specific data requirements is important for assessing their scalability across diverse processes on the factory floor. We investigate whether video pretraining can support reliable industrial manipulation with limited action data. We propose FLUX-mimic, a video-action model, motivated by the potential of video pretraining to capture motion and interaction patterns that enable sample-efficient training. FLUX-mimic is built on a backbone pretrained on large-scale web and industrial task data, and an action expert to generate robot trajectories. We train our policy by joint video-action training. Across four automotive manufacturing benchmark tasks and two bimanual robot platforms, FLUX-mimic outperforms all considered baselines on the dexterous robot platform and matches pi_{0.5}'s average success rate on the gripper-based robot platform. On a deformable part kitting task, FLUX-mimic reaches 77.5% success with as little as two hours of task-specific action-labeled demonstrations.

Abstract: GPT-6 Astra can drive a robot arm directly through a fixed harness, and scores of such robot-use agents on RoboDojo have risen quickly since. Reported systems differ in harness and test subset and often change several designs at once, so it is hard to tell which choices produce the gains. We take these designs apart and test them in one framework, along the harness around the model, the action interface, the reuse of experience and the scene geometry given to the model. A lean harness that keeps the full episode history and stops each motion early goes a long way. Reusing experience changes how the model acts but is not a free lunch, and scene geometry helps most, even when depth is predicted from RGB alone. JustRUA starts from the official L3 harness and keeps only what matters most. Even with no learned policy, self-improvement loop or task-specific tuning, this simple harness raises the score on the RoboDojo simulation benchmark substantially, from 29.7 to 50.3.

</details>

Abstract: Motion imitation enables humanoid robots to acquire whole-body skills such as walking and dancing. However, fine-tuning an entire policy for a new skill can affect existing behaviors, while training a separate policy for each skill increases training and maintenance costs. We present PERA, a method for learning new skills by reusing existing motion capabilities. PERA freezes a pretrained mixture-of-experts policy and trains an additional task expert and its routing score using task rewards or teacher-action supervision. In Unitree G1 simulation, each added task module contains approximately 1.03% of the parameters of the base actor. Built on our pretrained policy, PERA successfully completes all ten new tasks. Disabling the expert restores the original computation path of the base policy, which still fully executes all 126 original motions. Enabling the expert also improves the execution of some dance motions not used to train that expert, indicating that its benefits extend beyond its training motions. Experiments with HoloMotion further demonstrate the applicability of PERA to different base policies.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-camera observations in off-road environments vary significantly in reliability, posing challenges when complementing degraded geometric constraints. Motivated by this, we propose Quality-aware View Selection Lidar-Vision Odometry Risk Mapping (QVS-LIVO RiskMap), which decouples visual processing for state estimation from that for semantic mapping. The front end evaluates camera observations through read-only pretracking on a shared lidar-inertial posterior, combining observation health with geometric complementarity. Channel health memory and switching hysteresis govern selection of at most one eligible view for direct photometric correction. The posterior is retained when no view is eligible or the correction fails acceptance. The mapping back end integrates semantic observations from all available views in a shared world grid. Vehicle-scale geometry constrains semantic risk corrections, while spatial and temporal evidence supports risk confirmation. The system also produces a local bird's-eye-view (BEV) risk map and a global 2.5D semantic-terrain risk map with obstacle instance voxels. On two field sequences, QVS-LIVO achieved horizontal root mean square errors of 0.910 m and 1.664 m. It reduced error by 43.3% relative to FAST-LIO2 on the degraded long-route sequence. Across 12 controlled camera-fault configurations involving dropout, black frames and 60 ms timestamp shifts, it maintained complete trajectories with errors of 1.54-2.23 m. When deployed onboard an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin, the system generates local and global maps at 9.94 Hz and 2.00 Hz, respectively, with a median local mapping latency of 32.4 ms. These runtime results demonstrate the real-time feasibility of selective visual state updates alongside multi-view semantic mapping under challenging off-road conditions. The code and related resources are available at: https://github.com/zihanPan-HIT/QVS-LIVO.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon manipulation under uncertainty remains a challenging problem in robotics. We present \ourmethod{}, an algorithm for Visibility-Aware Task and Motion Planning. \ourmethod{} extends existing deterministic task and motion planning solvers to partially observable object rearrangement tasks, while still being able to take advantage of the efficiency and compositional generalization over long horizons of the base solver. This extension interleaves sensing actions into a plan skeleton that assumed full observability, and uses a novel persistent object-based memory to fuse observations into object mesh hypotheses and an observed occupancy map. We evaluate \ourmethod{} on real-world tabletop rearrangement tasks which feature heavy occlusion and partial visibility and demonstrate that it outperforms baselines that do not perform active perception or have persistent memory.

</details>

Abstract: Many robotic applications involve scenarios where multiple robots interact and the decisions of one robot influence the behavior of others. This paper presents a quadratic programming (QP)-based feedback controller for multi-robot conflict resolution with input constraints. Safety is enforced using coupled Control Barrier Function (CBF), while a yielding matrix encodes how robots influence each other. Unlike existing methods with fixed or hard priorities, the proposed framework enables yielding preferences while guaranteeing deadlock-free operation by design. Sufficient conditions are established for the controller to be locally Lipschitz. The optimal CBF sign- indefinite slack is shown to admit a finite uniform bound, which is compensated through an enlarged CBF safety margin to obtain a forward invariant set contained within the desired safe set. The analysis also identifies an irreducible slack component due to finite maximum deceleration that cannot be eliminated by increasing the slack penalty. Finally, sufficient conditions on the yielding matrix and nominal controller are derived to exclude deadlock states. Simulations demonstrate adaptive coordination across yielding preferences and robustness to robot failures.

</details>

Abstract: Generating diverse robot videos requires control over scene geometry, robot motion, and viewpoints. End-to-end video generation offers limited direct access to these variables, making targeted edits and reuse across tasks difficult. We present BWM-Forge, a code-driven framework for controllable video generation in robotics. Given robot assets and a task description, the framework accepts an initial image, an image with motion data, or a human demonstration video. A coding agent constructs editable Blender scene programs that specify objects, robot motion, and cameras. The resulting proxy videos provide structural and motion references for a video generation model, which adds visual detail. A multi-agent harness coordinates input processing, scene construction, review, and rendering. Automated evaluation and user feedback support iterative refinement, while an experience repository stores and retrieves reusable modeling and rendering rules. This design supports multiple robot embodiments, synchronized viewpoints, and appearance variation from a shared scene program. We evaluate the framework on 15 tasks covering five robot types. Compared with LingBot-World Base Cam on these tasks, BWM-Forge achieves higher aesthetic quality, imaging quality, subject and background consistency, motion smoothness, and RoboJudge-9B physical adherence and instruction alignment scores. The baseline scores higher in dynamic degree. Qualitative cases illustrate viewpoint control, scene variation, feedback-driven edits, and experience reuse across scenes. These results demonstrate a practical approach to generating controllable robot videos through editable, executable scene representations.

</details>

Abstract: Navigation agents use a large language model (LLM) planner to call a vision-language-action (VLA) model. Each episode needs the LLM, adding cost and delay. We distill successful agent experience into the VLA instead of distilling the planner. The VLA then navigates alone at test time. Agent sessions are not VLA demonstrations. They include motions driven by planner tools and sub-instructions, along with detours. We present H2E, a task-level experience distillation method that converts these sessions into VLA training data. Every motion becomes a VLA action under the original task instruction, with the resulting poses as targets. H2E masks the loss on steps that move away from the goal. It labels every visited state within 1 m of the goal as a stop. We then update the VLA with low-rank adaptation. The VLA learns to stop at the goal and find goals the base model never reaches. On all 3,189 REVERIE-CE val-unseen episodes, H2E raises the success rate of an 8B VLA from 35.0% to 44.9%.

</details>

Abstract: Flexible resistive force sensors are useful for wearable, rehabilitation, and human--machine interfaces, but their performance depends on sensing-material architecture and fabrication. This work presents a parameterized Voltera NOVA-based direct-ink-writing workflow using printed silver electrodes with multilayer Velostat sensing elements on PET and TPU substrates, together with an exploratory fully printed carbon architecture. Velostat sensors containing 1, 2, 4, and 6 sensing layers exhibited strongly nonlinear force--voltage responses, with the greatest sensitivity at low forces. Best-fit straight-line $R^2$ values ranged from 0.475 to 0.641, area-based hysteresis from 3.90\% to 7.47\%, and 5-min voltage drift from 1.9\% to 7.3\%, with no consistent monotonic relationship between layer count and these metrics. Selected PET6 and TPU6 devices showed less than 0.05~V change in peak output between the first and final 100 cycles of a 10,000-cycle test. Printed-carbon devices demonstrated the feasibility of a more fully printed architecture, but configurations with six or fewer layers saturated below approximately 0.5~N or were nonfunctional, while the eight-layer device responded primarily below approximately 2~N. These results establish the feasibility of the printed-electrode Velostat workflow while identifying printed sensing materials as a key area for further development.

</details>

Abstract: We study robot navigation in indoor environments from fixed exocentric cameras (infrastructure-mounted, external to the robot). We show that standard Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which succeeds with onboard cameras, fails to learn this navigation end-to-end in our setup. This failure results from the perspective mismatch between exocentric observations and egocentric action and goal formulations, which is too complex to resolve from the reward signal alone. To address this, we propose Viewpoint-Invariant Navigation from Exocentric Cameras (VINE). VINE uses an obstacle map estimation network, trained with supervised learning, that takes exocentric images, camera pose, robot pose, and room dimensions as input and produces a viewpoint-invariant obstacle map. This map can be used either by a PPO policy or by a planner such as A*. VINE's map estimation performs better than perspective mapping approaches, handles cameras placed across a wide range of positions and orientations without view-specific tuning, requires only approximate camera pose estimates (noise with a standard deviation of 30cm per axis in x, y, and z and about 20° of rotation error on average), is robust to intrinsic variation, and extends to two-camera setups with partial coverage. Despite operating from cameras randomized in position during evaluation, VINE surpasses the navigation success rate of PPO with an onboard camera in simulated environments.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents an overview of the integrated technologies developed for the Open Platform League at RoboCup@Home 2026 in Incheon. A vision system integrated with learning-based, training-free models enables robust object recognition for both known and unknown objects. We introduce a RoomPlan-based method that semi-automatically registers furniture geometry and labels to generate semantic maps. Additionally, a large language model-based planner interprets the instructions recognized by Whisper and generates primitive skill sequences for execution. A reservoir-computing framework serves as a lightweight temporal-processing model for action recognition. Its field-programmable gate array-based implementation enables low-power, real-time operation. This is key to removing operational time constraints for service robots. Open-source releases, including task environments, dataset tools, and navigation frameworks, enhance reproducibility, reuse, and continuous progress within the RoboCup@Home community.

Abstract: Robots handle much more than rigid objects: towels, cables, water, sand. The simulators used to train robots at scale are strongest on rigid bodies, and adding another material usually means a second engine and hand-written glue between the two. We present Alloy, an open-source physics engine for robot learning in which rigid robots, cloth, cables, liquids, granular material and soft bodies share one scene. Each object is described once; Alloy assigns it to a solver suited to its material, couples the solvers inside every control step, and runs the scene in many parallel worlds on one GPU, or on a CPU. Every world carries a health grade, to help tell a failure of the physics from a failure of the policy. Alloy is built on Newton, MuJoCo Warp and Warp, and it is written to be driven by coding agents: its documentation ships inside the package, every error names its fix, and the engine is exposed as tools over the Model Context Protocol. In a cold test, agents that had never seen Alloy installed a pre-release from the package index and trained policies for walking, cable handling and pouring on a single GPU. We also report how throughput grows with the number of worlds, depth-camera throughput, agreement with closed-form references, and a reliability test over a thousand worlds.

</details>


### [431] [Memory-Efficient Parallel Policy Learning for Collapse-Reactive Retreat via Hybrid Terrain Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16337)

Abstract: At disaster sites and on unstable slopes, robot contact can collapse uninspected terrain, requiring retreat upon support loss. Massively parallel reinforcement learning of collapse-reactive retreat requires independent state--action experiences. However, on shared rigid-body collapsible terrain, one robot's collapse alters others' contacts without direct interaction. We call this indirect interference. Duplicating terrain objects prevents this but is memory-intensive, limiting parallel sampling. Our key insight is that policy learning does not necessarily require rigid-body collapse modeling, which is feasible but computationally expensive; collapse-induced contact responses can suffice. We therefore propose a memory-efficient parallel policy-learning framework for collapse-reactive retreat via hybrid terrain modeling. Hybrid terrain modeling combines rigid-body simulation of non-collapsible terrain with virtual contact forces for collapsible regions. A State-Multiplexed Contact Model shares terrain geometry and a contact-response law while maintaining environment-specific collapse states, enabling independent experiences without terrain objects for collapsible regions. The framework trains a proprioceptive policy for both normal locomotion and collapse-reactive retreat. It supports over 100 times as many parallel environments as fine-resolution rigid-block terrain within the same GPU memory limit. This scalability and improved sample efficiency enable over 50 times faster policy convergence with comparable retreat performance. Sim-to-sim and real-robot experiments demonstrate retreat under multiple collapse conditions. A supplementary video is available at: https://youtu.be/_RrhwjzEPE0

</details>

Abstract: Reusing a demonstrated gait can reduce the work of specifying the same motion for each biped. A video captures the timing of a step, but each robot must execute it with its own limbs and actuators. MotionSkill recovers a timed reference once from monocular video and adapts it to each target in simulation. It aligns root motion and body landmarks in a common frame and records which landmarks are available at each instant. At each source timestamp, the mapper preserves limb directions, uses the target segment lengths, and solves inverse kinematics under joint limits and closure constraints. In MuJoCo, we train a separate residual policy for each of ten bipeds on its mapped reference from one four-second walking clip. Each policy uses the measured robot state to correct joint commands during contact. From initial states held out during training, MotionSkill completed 98% of trials on the original video and 26% with the upper body occluded for one second.

</details>


### [433] [Input-output characterization of robotic grasping: Connecting design parameters to grasp behavior through the admittance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16507)

Abstract: Low-impedance grippers make the dynamics of a grasp matter. Current grasp metrics, however, are static: they describe how a held object resists constant loads, not how it responds to time-varying ones. To quantify this, we rely on small-signal analysis to characterize a grasp by its admittance, the transfer matrix relating load perturbations to object displacements around an equilibrium. This transfer matrix recovers the stiffness and stability of the grasp at zero frequency and captures its natural frequencies and peak gain under time-varying loads. We apply this characterization to a planar two-finger gripper under proportional-derivative control. We show that two grasps of the same object in the same position and with the same contact forces can differ in stiffness by two orders of magnitude. Likewise, two grasps that share the same stiffness can exhibit very different resonances. Nonlinear simulations confirm the effectiveness of our small-signal analysis in capturing the dynamic behavior of grasping.

</details>


### [434] [ManiLadder: Benchmarking Robot Manipulation Through a Categorized and Multi-Difficulty Level Task Ladder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16374)

Abstract: Recent advances in robotic manipulation have enabled increasingly complex and dexterous behaviors, yet existing benchmarks still focus on simple pick-and-place tasks and lack a systematic framework for measuring progress in robot learning along the axis of task difficulty. We present ManiLadder, a large-scale and organized simulation benchmark for robotic manipulation. ManiLadder contains 112 carefully designed tasks stratified into 4 difficulty levels, with 8 categories in each level, spanning rigid-body and deformable-object manipulation, single-arm and dual-arm settings, and gripper and dexterous-hand end-effectors. To calibrate difficulty, we fix the data amount the same for every task and use a unified flow- matching imitation learning policy under the same training and evaluation protocols, then use its evaluation success rate as an operational empirical difficulty signal. All tasks are iteratively refined until they fall into target difficulty ranges, which are then verified across different random seeds, policies, and agentic learning systems. We further analyze the task properties, compare different policy performances and training settings, analyze task transfer and data scaling behaviors, and conduct real-world experiments showing that the designed tasks preserve their difficulty ordering beyond simulation. ManiLad- der provides a calibrated ladder for measuring, comparing, and advancing robotic manipulation capabilities. More results are available at https://maniladder-benchmark.github.io/.

</details>


### [435] [Remember What Changes the Odds: Critic-Gated Event Memory for Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16386)

Abstract: In long-horizon manipulation, a robot may need to take different actions under identical observations, depending on task progress and the outcomes of earlier attempts. We refer to this ambiguity as decision aliasing. Resolving it requires the policy to maintain memory of its execution history. To reduce the cost of processing full observation histories, recent methods build compact event memories using perceptual, semantic, or kinematic cues. However, these criteria may miss events that matter for task success. We instead propose an outcome-grounded principle: remember events that substantially change the predicted probability of eventual success. Following this principle, we introduce CGEM, a critic-gated event memory that uses a critic to select events with large estimated changes in success probability. Positive and negative changes are encoded as progress and failure records, respectively. Failure records are discarded once the corresponding subtask is completed. In a ten-subtask diagnostic environment with exactly computed success probabilities, CGEM achieves 88.0% success, compared with 0.0% for both a 36-step observation window and a kinematic event memory. Removing failure records reduces success to 1.7%.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have become a powerful foundation for robotic scene understanding, task planning, and language-conditioned control. A natural question follows: can these models also decode what a human operator intends while teleoperating a robot, without any explicit instruction? We formulate this as hierarchical intent prediction, jointly inferring the ongoing atomic action and the broader mission it serves from a short visual teleoperation history. To study this, we introduce VI AMO (Vision Intention Action and Mission Ongoing), a dataset of 6,600 episodes from eleven robot sources with 13,653 temporally aligned action annotations and 9,702 mission annotations. Large proprietary models struggle with this problem: on held-out episodes, GPT-4o reaches Sentence-BERT similarities of only 0.563 and 0.470 for action and mission, and GPT-5.6 Luna achieves only 0.581 and 0.554. Fine-tuning a VLM on VI AMO yields our Vision-Intention Model (VIM), which raises these scores to 0.825 and 0.821 and still reaches 0.726 and 0.717 on the 35.4% of test actions with both labels absent from training. Finally, as a downstream application, VIM's predicted mission enables a vision-language-action (VLA) policy to take over from the operator mid-task in a real-world environment unseen during VIM fine-tuning, reducing human teleoperation time by 34.6% relative to GPT-4o and 39.8% relative to GPT-5.6 Luna, while cutting intent-prediction latency by roughly 70% on average.

</details>

Abstract: Whole-body motion tracking lets a humanoid robot follow reference motions, but training a tracking policy for a library of motions has usually taken hours of GPU time. SuperMimic showed that the policy can instead be trained by backpropagating through a world model learned from parallel simulation, and InstantTrack added Muon updates, a residual policy around the reference and an averaged deployed policy. We build on both and present BlinkMimic, a recipe that makes this training faster. On a simulated Unitree G1, BlinkMimic trains one policy for all 40 LAFAN1 clips in 3.4 minutes on a single RTX 4090, measured until three consecutive checkpoints reach a normalized return of at least 0.82 with no failed clip (mean of three seeds). We split training time into the cost of an iteration, the iterations needed to fit the motions, and the iterations needed to remove the last failures, and assign each component, inherited or new, to one of these terms. The new components are a step size that decreases once training has progressed, resets directed by failures of the deployed policy under deployment conditions, and GPU graph capture of the whole iteration. A leave-one-out ablation with three seeds per component shows which term each component affects, and further measurements of the gradient, the update rule and the failure cases explain these effects. Code and per-seed results are available at https://github.com/fuyichen0725/blinkmimic.

</details>


### [438] [VOKE: Visual Object Kinematics Estimator for Dexterous In-Hand Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16423)
*Shengjia Zhu,Wenqiang Xu,Lin Shao*

Abstract: Vision-based dexterous in-hand manipulation requires knowing not only where an object is, but also how it moves. However, most robotic manipulation systems rely on off-the-shelf visual perception models whose outputs are limited to static object masks, geometry, or 6D poses, leaving downstream controllers to infer object dynamics implicitly from noisy visual observations. We present VOKE, a kinematics-aware perception framework that augments existing object and hand-object pose estimators to recover a complete object kinematic state comprising pose, linear and angular velocity, and linear and angular acceleration. VOKE contains two core perception modules and a downstream application study. First, a Pluggable Kinematics Module (PKM) attaches to the output of a standard pose estimator and predicts the object's linear and angular velocity and acceleration from temporal visual features. Second, a Kinematics-guided Pose Refinement Module (KPRM) converts this motion vector into a second-order, motion-prior reference pose and refines the per-frame pose estimates. We evaluate VOKE on DexYCB and OakInk, where it consistently improves object pose estimation across three different backbones and substantially reduces velocity and acceleration estimation error compared with pose differencing. As a downstream application, we use Kinematics-Augmented Reinforcement Learning (KARL) to compare a kinematics-augmented policy with a pose-history baseline. After training the policies in simulation, we transfer the kinematics-augmented policy to the physical robot without policy fine-tuning, demonstrating the complete perception-to-control pathway in the target domain.

</details>

Abstract: Learning whole-body mobile manipulation requires demonstrations that coordinate bimanual actions with locomotion across extended workspaces. However, existing interfaces trade off direct robot control, perception and action alignment, and natural operator mobility, making such demonstrations difficult to collect. We introduce HALO, a human-aligned data collection system for whole-body mobile manipulation. The system aligns the operator's perception and action space with the robot while preserving direct physical feedback and natural locomotion. Operators guide the robot end-effectors through kinesthetic teaching and move a low-resistance platform without the deployed mobile base while performing bimanual manipulation. This interface enables more natural whole-body demonstrations, but the close human-robot coupling introduces discrepancies between the demonstration and deployment settings. We address these discrepancies with deployment-aligned demonstration processing, which calibrates and temporally aligns platform motion with the deployed base response and masks and inpaints demonstrator hands in recorded observations. Compared with Mobile ALOHA and VR teleoperation, HALO enables up to 2.9x faster demonstration collection while maintaining comparable or higher success rates. Policies trained with HALO perform precise manipulation tasks, including zip-tie fastening, and achieve a 73.3% success rate on a six-stage long-horizon mobile manipulation task involving repeated coordinated base and arm motion. Project page: https://robbyant-research.github.io/Lingbot-ALOHA/

</details>

Abstract: Micro aerial vehicles that operate without GPS often run SLAM on ARM-based edge boards, yet there is little published data on how standard RGB-D SLAM systems behave on such hardware. We benchmark RTAB-Map and ORB-SLAM3 on the Raspberry Pi 5 using six TUM RGB-D sequences at three image resolutions, recording tracking coverage, trajectory error, time per frame, memory, CPU load and temperature for every run, and we profile both systems at the stage and function level. RTAB-Map was configured to process and keep every frame, so its back end runs on every frame rather than at its default rate of 1 Hz. On the sequences that every configuration tracked, its front end takes 38-74 ms per frame and its full pipeline 150-462 ms, compared with 13-45 ms and 26-62 ms for ORB-SLAM3. RTAB-Map's main cost is optimizing a pose graph that keeps growing, because its working memory is unbounded by default; ORB-SLAM3's local bundle adjustment costs about as much as its tracking but runs in a parallel thread. At quarter resolution, both systems lose tracking on most sequences, and runs that lose tracking finish sooner and report lower error on the poses they keep, so time and accuracy must be read alongside coverage. Timing is repeatable to within 1-5%, but trajectory error varies by up to 27% between identical runs, so the accuracy differences between the two systems are inconclusive. No thermal throttling occurred.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic manipulation often requires both spatial scene understanding and task history. However, many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely primarily on current observations, without explicitly integrating spatial structure and task history. Appearance-based memories can be sensitive to occlusion and viewpoint changes, while spatial representations of the current scene alone do not preserve how the scene has changed during task execution. We introduce STVLA, a framework that incorporates features from a pretrained visual geometry foundation model into temporal memory alongside visual representations. This allows the policy to recall past scene structure as context for current action decisions. To select informative history, STVLA uses a mathematically grounded scoring rule that balances relevance to the current observation with complementary evidence from memory. Across evaluations on LIBERO, LIBERO-Mem, MIKASA-Robo, and SimplerEnv Bridge, STVLA achieves the highest average success rate among the compared methods on each benchmark. On real-world block stacking, object organization, and phone packaging, it improves success over $π_{0.5}$ by $58.33$, $30.00$, and $35.00$ percentage points, respectively. These results support integrating scene geometry into temporal memory to improve manipulation requiring both spatial precision and task history. Project website: \href{https://stvla.github.io/}{https://stvla.github.io/}

</details>

Abstract: Frontier models such as GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 can now plan and call tools, yet they still struggle to drive real robots through long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation, blind while the arm moves and while they think. We present Reins, a harness in which an off-the-shelf model operates a dual-arm robot with 3D perception tools and atomic actions that report contact and grip. The model chains actions in one call, each with a condition that must hold, stopping at the first that fails; a second instance, the narrator, watches the cameras continuously and reports what changed with every result. From images, Reins also builds a simulation-ready twin of the scene for continual policy learning. With natural-language prompts and no task-specific code, GPT-6.1 Sol succeeds in 88% of real episodes on five tasks; without the harness, it fails every shell game, taking 8 times as long. Three frontier models perform alike but differ in tracking a scene that changes while the actor thinks. Code will be released upon publication.

</details>

Abstract: Actions in expert demonstrations exhibit temporal structure. An ordered-versus-shuffled analysis across five environments confirms this dependence. We ask whether action proposals that better reflect expert continuity can improve visual world-model planning. Rather than imposing generic smoothness, we learn continuations from the temporal structure of demonstrated actions. Our locality-gated deep-kernel Gaussian-process prior, LG-DKGP, combines recent and longer-range executed action history, predicts a neural continuation, and learns a temporal Gaussian-process (GP) residual. A training-derived exploration scale initializes search around the corrected mean; a frozen world model evaluates progress toward the goal. Training first fits the neural mean, then jointly optimizes the prior with the GP residual using expert future actions. The resulting planner achieves 87.07\% macro success across five simulated tasks spanning native and fragment-level goal attainment, compared with 71.03\% for INTACT, 61.66\% for PRISM, and 58.66\% for LeWM. Component interventions and expert-relative action-structure comparisons support demonstration-informed continuations and reveal task-dependent effects of visual conditioning, history length, and planning horizon.

</details>


### [445] [NSDL: Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor for Multi-Robot Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16671)
*Xincheng Tan,Ke Wang,Yining Di,Minfeng Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Abstract: Planning for a heterogeneous robot team from a scene image and an instruction involves two kinds of decisions. Some must be judged from the scene: which goals are already met, how the task splits, and which robot does what. Others follow from these and can be computed: prerequisite actions, handoffs between robots, and timing. We argue that each kind should go to the component suited to it: a small vision-language model learns the first, and a deterministic solver computes the second. In NSDL (Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor), the trained model writes its judgments as a plan sketch, which lists the goals that still need action and the intended steps in order, each with a robot. The solver expands the sketch into an executable joint plan. The model calls two tools, a detector for evidence and the solver, and is trained with the solver in the loop. We evaluate on the public VIKI-L2 benchmark and on LOCUS-Bench, a new benchmark that grades the two kinds of difficulty separately. NSDL outperforms end-to-end planners and formalize-then-plan baselines on both benchmarks and with three backbones from 3B to 8B; the 7B model reaches 97.0% out-of-distribution accuracy on VIKI-L2. Ordering and scheduling errors nearly disappear once the solver writes the structure; the remaining failures are misjudged states, and most goals the model misses are ones it never lists. Code, model weights, and LOCUS-Bench are publicly available at https://github.com/Xincheng-Tan/NSDL.

</details>

Abstract: Smartphone-distracted pedestrians deviate from social norms unpredictably and cannot be relied upon to yield to approaching robots -- a safety challenge that existing navigation methods are poorly equipped to handle, since they treat all pedestrians uniformly regardless of attention state. We propose an attention-aware navigation framework that classifies each pedestrian as attentive or distracted in real-time using 3D skeletal features and a Random Forest classifier, then dynamically widens cost-map safety margins around distracted individuals to drive proactive avoidance. A within-subjects user study with 12 participants across 252 trials demonstrates significant improvements in perceived smoothness (p = 0.003) and predictability (p = 0.034) over both a LiDAR-only baseline and a trajectory-prediction-only method, without any increase in travel time. Notably, trajectory prediction alone does not improve subjective navigation quality unless coupled with attention-state-aware cost adjustment -- confirming that appropriately responding to who is in front of the robot can matter more than trajectory smoothness alone.

</details>

Abstract: Generalist manipulation policies can benefit from the complementary experience of humans, real robots, and simulated agents. However, differences in morphology, sensing, and control interfaces make it difficult to learn effectively from their combined demonstrations. We introduce EgoX, an egocentric cross-embodiment learning framework that organizes heterogeneous demonstrations through semantically corresponding observation and action modalities. EgoX pretrains a Modularized Cross-Embodiment Transformer with Embodiment Dreaming (MXT+) on this mixture and fine-tunes it on target-embodiment demonstrations. Within each embodiment, MXT+ assigns a dedicated tokenizer to each observation modality and a dedicated expert to each action modality, with a Transformer trunk shared across embodiments. A training-only embodiment-dreaming objective predicts latent representations of future visual and proprioceptive states, encouraging the shared trunk to model each embodiment's dynamics. Across 13 task-embodiment pairs spanning five tasks and six robot embodiments, including five physical robots and one simulated humanoid, cross-embodiment pretraining raises MXT+'s average normalized task progress from 0.63 to 0.80 over training from scratch. The gains are particularly pronounced under out-of-distribution conditions, where average task progress increases from 0.53 to 0.77. Ablations demonstrate the benefits of broader pretraining mixtures, modular interfaces, and embodiment dreaming, with dreaming producing more similar latent geometry across embodiments. Project page: egox-dream.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous greenhouse management increasingly requires robotic systems that integrate perception, navigation, environmental regulation, and plant-level intervention. This paper presents AgriSense, a framework combining an Isaac Sim virtual greenhouse, a quadruped mobile manipulator, RGB and LiDAR-inertial sensing, environmental control, and supervised vision-language decision support. Frozen DINOv2 and BERT encoders, together with structured environmental inputs, support joint prediction of twelve environmental references, a target zone, and an intervention flag. Checkpoint replay reproduces 41/43 zone decisions. Across five seeds on a source-grouped test set, the full, image-removed, and language-removed configurations each achieve 43/44 correct zone decisions, providing no evidence of incremental multimodal zone-accuracy improvement. The full model achieves mean absolute errors of 0.249 degrees C for temperature, 2.030 percentage points for humidity, and 0.997 percentage points for soil moisture. Sensor-only inputs match full-model zone accuracy, while language-only inputs achieve 68.64%. External real-image benchmarks reveal distribution-shift limitations. Five offline Point-LIO routes yield rigidly aligned trajectory errors of 5.22-17.32 mm. Of 40 virtual-arm targets, 32 satisfy geometric acceptance criteria and eight are rejected at boundaries. Six long-horizon climate cases and fault protocols characterize integration limits. Navigation and integrated treatment rely on simulator-state inputs; physical greenhouse performance and agronomic treatment efficacy remain unvalidated. The contribution is an evaluated decision-support and robotic-intervention framework for a virtual greenhouse.

Abstract: Recent advances in frontier models have pushed the boundaries of agentic robot control, enabling agents to perform complex robotic tasks through reasoning and interaction. However, repeated calls to these models during execution can substantially increase task completion time. Code as Policies (CaP) offers a route for coding agents to acquire physical skills through experience and retain them in executable programs, allowing the reasoning invested in development to be reused across runs. Developing reliable code policies while limiting the cost of physical experimentation remains a central challenge. We investigate human guidance as a source of priors for feasible actions and simulation as an auxiliary environment for exploring and testing candidate policies. Our findings favor robot demonstrations available on request and simulation used selectively during real-world policy refinement. These findings inform ACE-CaP-0, an agentic recipe that couples policy revision with experience acquisition through Demonstrations on Request and Simulation on Demand. Across eight real-world manipulation tasks spanning alignment, multi-object handling, and bimanual coordination, the resulting frozen programs achieve 92.1\% success (221/240 trials). Mean execution times across tasks range from 0.97 to 2.27 minutes. The programs execute with runtime perception and control, without a human or coding agent in the loop. These results highlight CaP's potential to turn experience into reliable, reusable physical skills.

</details>


### [450] [Informative Path Planning with Gaussian Processes: Error Bounds, Algorithms, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16877)

Abstract: Accurate environmental monitoring relies on Informative Path Planning (IPP) to deploy autonomous systems that maximize information gain. While Gaussian Processes (GPs) provide a robust mathematical foundation for modeling the environment, contemporary IPP approaches remain hindered by heuristic operational constraints and the lack of simultaneous theoretical guarantees for worst-case estimation error and approximation quality. Rather than introducing yet another heuristic, this paper presents a rigorous theoretical analysis of existing error bounds and algorithms to further develop the mathematical foundation for IPP.
  By evaluating probabilistic and deterministic bounds on reconstruction error, we establish that predictive variance is the theoretically ideal optimization objective for GP-based IPP. Furthermore, we analyze GreedyCover, a recent approach for optimizing predictive variance, and demonstrate that its near-optimal approximation guarantee matches the fundamental hardness-of-approximation limit for the problem. Comprehensive benchmarking confirms that this approach delivers superior estimation accuracy and computational efficiency compared to prominent information-theoretic objectives and optimization methods used in IPP. Finally, this theoretical analysis establishes a rigorous, near-optimal foundation for discrete offline IPP under bounded uncertainty constraints, offering clear and principled guidance for future work in continuous-space and online formulations.

</details>


### [451] [CT-SGAL-LIO: Causal Continuous Time Refinement of Discrete LiDAR Inertial Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16918)
*Nam Tran,Thu Tran,Toan Nguyen,William J Beksi,Tuan Dang*

Abstract: State-of-the-art LiDAR-inertial odometry methods, including those on richer state manifolds, achieve strong global accuracy, yet their pose stream alternates between propagation and correction and can oscillate over short horizons even when globally accurate. Most existing methods follow these approaches: they either modify the filter to enable faster updates or estimate a continuous-time trajectory from raw LiDAR and inertial data. The latter involves extra optimization, delaying state updates and becoming less robust during rapid rotations or accelerations. We propose CT-SGAL-LIO, a continuous-time refinement module that smooths the posterior pose stream of a discrete SGal(3) estimator without modifying the estimator itself. The key novelty is to perform continuous-time trajectory refinement directly on the projected SGal(3) posterior: we fit a cubic trajectory over a bounded window of recent poses and evaluate it at a small fixed lag. This places the query time within the observed interval, so the refined pose is obtained by interpolation rather than extrapolation, while preserving causal operation. CT-SGAL-LIO improves relative trajectory accuracy on 17 sequences from three public benchmarks. Across 17 sequences from GRAND-TOUR, MCD, and GEODE, CT-SGAL-LIO improves short-term motion accuracy while largely preserving global accuracy. RTE improves by up to 70% on GRAND-TOUR and on all seven GEODE sequences, with 9.9-51.1% reductions. A 19.5-min R-Campus run also reduces posterior jumps by 85.4% with only a 4.7 mm change in the end-to-start gap. We plan to release our source code and results to support community validation and reproducibility.

</details>


### [452] [Maximizing LLM Utility While Managing LLM Fallibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17020)
*Ekta U. Samani,Christopher G. Atkeson*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can supply autonomous systems with plans, predictions, and judgments, but these contributions are sometimes wrong. This review asks how a system can maximize an LLM's utility while managing its fallibility. Our answer is to adapt the system's reliance on the LLM as evidence about the LLM's performance accumulates. Systems built from imperfect components, such as noisy sensors and faulty software, have long worked in this way, and the classical methods they use can be applied to LLM-based systems as well. A system can change its reliance on an LLM in several ways, depending on the LLM's role and on the other components in the system. Each change needs an estimate of the LLM's reliability, and the LLM's role and the kind of contribution it makes determine what to estimate. Classical methods from decision theory, estimation, and online learning then turn these estimates into reliance decisions. These decisions can reduce how often LLM errors affect the system. However, some errors matter even when they are rare. An independently verified mechanism can prevent such errors from being executed. Without one, adapting reliance using reliability estimates can still keep their risk within a bound. Studies on adapting reliance on LLMs are emerging but limited, and the reviewed comparisons suggest that adapting reliance can improve system performance.

</details>


### [453] [SALA: Semantically Aligned Latent Actions Enable Dual-Axis Scaling of Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17110)
*Jiachun Jin,Zetong Zhou,Haonan Wang,Xiao Yang,Jun Zhu,Zhijie Deng*

Abstract: Embodied foundation models hinge on diverse robot interaction data, which are costly to collect. Egocentric human videos offer a complementary source of experience, and latent action models (LAMs) provide a popular means of leveraging these videos for policy supervision. Compared with world-action models (WAMs), LAMs can offer greater training efficiency and potentially lower inference costs by avoiding explicit pixel prediction during policy learning and execution. However, most existing LAMs learn latent actions solely through video reconstruction, which can cause their representations to cluster by visual appearance rather than by the underlying actions. As a result, they struggle to align human actions with their robot counterparts. To bridge the gap, we introduce SALA, which emphasizes establishing the semantic correspondence for latent actions. Without requiring paired data, we use the language descriptions of unpaired human and robot clips to estimate semantic similarities and supervise cross-embodiment correspondence. Specifically, we translate these similarities into soft neighborhood structures that guide human latent actions toward semantically related robot latents, while robot action reconstruction grounds the latent space in control. The resulting shared latent action space provides a unified supervision signal for training an embodied foundation model on both human and robot videos. Notably, latent action supervision enables scaling along two axes: (1) pre-training on increasing amounts of general human video consistently reduces robot action prediction error on downstream tasks, and (2) post-training on increasing amounts of task-specific human video substantially improves real-world robot task success rates.

</details>


### [454] [FORGE: Force-Aware Multi-Granularity Control for Efficient Humanoid Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17142)

Abstract: Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinated locomotion, balance regulation, and manipulation under frequent contacts, making teleoperated demonstration collection difficult: a practical controller must simultaneously provide precision, stability, and contact awareness. We propose FORGE, a force-aware multi-granularity control system with a coarse-to-fine command space spanning velocity navigation, planar foot placement, wrist-pose tracking, and optional force-conditioning inputs. Policy training requires no full-body human motion trajectories or joint-angle references; deployment consumes only sparse bilateral wrist poses relative to the pelvis, coarse motion commands, and optional desired wrist-load inputs. For sustained contact, FORGE combines a momentum observer with a learned correction trained using simulator contact-force supervision. At deployment, it estimates low-frequency, wrist-equivalent interaction forces from proprioception and reconstructed low-level arm PD torques, without wrist force/torque sensors. A quasi-static virtual-target loop uses this estimate and an optional desired applied-force input to adapt the wrist target. A frozen locomotion prior and asymmetric residual actions stabilize manipulation-induced whole-body corrections. Experiments evaluate unified-control stability, task-space tracking, quasi-static wrist-equivalent force estimation, squeezing forces during sustained contact with a rigid box, and downstream diffusion-policy execution of a squat-and-lift task across payload conditions. Website: https://bb0928.github.io/forge/.

</details>


### [455] [Inner Product Regularization for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17159)
*Benjamin A. Christie,Tianyu Qiu,Amy H. Choi,David Fridovich-Keil,Dylan P. Losey*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Robots that learn from demonstrations should extrapolate their policy despite state perturbations such as sensor noise, scene variation, and compounding errors. One approach to this robustness is regularization. Prior regularizers consider each state on its own and limit how sensitive the policy is to any perturbation at that state. By contrast, we explore how the policy's output shifts when given the same perturbation at different states. Our insight is that, locally, the robot should respond to state perturbations in a consistent manner: e.g., if changing an irrelevant object position does not impact the policy at one state, that same change should not shift the policy output at a nearby state. Applying this insight, we develop inner product regularization (InneR). We show that the inner product on action space induces an inner product on state space through the policy Jacobian (the Fisher information for stochastic policies), and that the null space of this inner product governs which state perturbations have a first-order effect on the policy. By clustering states whose inner products are aligned, averaging the inner products within each cluster, and minimizing the smallest eigenvalues of that average, InneR shapes the learned policy so that nearby states share a common null space. InneR enforces consistent responses to perturbations without limiting policy expressiveness, and can be appended to existing imitation learning pipelines for deterministic or stochastic policies. In simulated experiments, InneR makes learned policies more robust to irrelevant state dimensions and shifts between training and test distributions, whereas norm penalties often perform worse than behavior cloning. On a real robot arm performing three manipulation tasks, InneR raises the average success rate of a diffusion policy from 18% to 57% without additional data.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied interaction requires an agent to integrate multimodal perception with verbal and nonverbal behaviors to effectively communicate its intentions, including speech, body motion, and facial expressions. We present XBot-Omni, a Thinker--Talker--Actor architecture that extends the Omni paradigm to robotic modalities, including body motion and facial expressions, with ``X'' representing the extensibility to robotic-specific modalities. The Thinker processes text, images, audio, and video to formulate contextually appropriate responses, which are then realized by the Talker and Actor through speech and coordinated bodily expressions, respectively. In this report, we innovatively introduce the concept of ``Actor'' as a dedicated component for embodied agents: a coordinated, streaming module that leverages the native representations of the Thinker and Talker within an end-to-end pipeline, introducing only modest latency overhead. This unified architecture enables multimodal generation conditioned on omni-modal inputs directly to prevent information loss in staged designs. We comprehensively evaluate XBot-Omni across seven benchmarks, demonstrating strong multimodal understanding capabilities on both general-domain and robot-centric videos, as well as high-quality, natural, and well-coordinated motion generation. Furthermore, our results validate its capability to generate embodied multimodal responses directly from omni inputs, meeting the diverse requirements of embodied interaction. XBot-Omni enables robots not only to perceive the omni world but also to express themselves through speech and bodily behaviors, extending Omni models from conversational interaction to embodied communication in physical environments.

</details>

Abstract: Language-guided UAV navigation has largely focused on long-horizon route planning, leaving short-range, object-relative flight maneuvers underexplored. To address this gap, we formulate Flying-on-a-Word (Flow), a task requiring a UAV to convert concise natural-language instruction, egocentric visual observation, and current state dynamics into continuous flight actions. Flow encompasses both primitive motion commands and complex object-interactive behaviors, assessing motion intent comprehension and spatial context grounding. To enable policy learning and closed-loop evaluation, we introduce UAV-Flow, a comprehensive dataset comprising 30,692 expert-piloted real-world trajectories across eight distinct motion categories, alongside 10,109 simulation trajectories in the UAV-Flow-Sim environment equipped with task-specific completion criteria. Furthermore, we develop FlowVLA, which undergoes imitation pretraining on mixed real-sim demonstrations and online post-training via Tail-Aware GRPO (TGRPO). By leveraging group reward distributions, TGRPO effectively reinforces high-reward action sequences. Evaluated on 273 held-out UAV-Flow-Sim episodes, pretrained FlowVLA achieves a mean success rate of 75.58%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 6.72 percentage points, which TGRPO post-training further elevates to 93.16% with superior sample efficiency. Physical UAV experiments demonstrate robust execution of object-relative commands in both indoor and outdoor real-world scenes. Together, Flow establishes a novel benchmark and learning framework for language-conditioned flight maneuvers.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-robot systems face the challenge of efficiently allocating teams of heterogeneous robots to tasks. The task allocation problem is complicated by collaborative interactions between robots where teams of robots develop emergent capabilities that enable them to complete tasks that would be inefficient or impossible for individual robots. To address these challenges, we present an iterative clustering algorithm for collaborative task allocation in heterogeneous multi-robot systems. This approach partitions the computationally intractable global optimization problem into smaller, tractable subproblems by iteratively forming clusters of robots and tasks, then optimizing assignments within each cluster. By ensuring robots remain clustered with their currently assigned tasks, we guarantee monotonic improvement in overall utility with each iteration. We analyze the convergence of the algorithm and characterize how cluster size constraints determine which suboptimal assignments could trap the algorithm. In simulation, iterative clustering consistently outperforms simulated annealing, and a group-based auction in both computation time and solution quality, and outperforms a hedonic game approach in solution quality.

</details>

Abstract: For robots to assist, serve, and collaborate with humans, they must not only interpret human requests conveyed through language and multimodal intentional cues, but also execute long-horizon tasks that interleave navigation and manipulation. In this paper, we introduce HEIR, a resource for studying how to Harness Egocentric Intent for Human-Robot Interaction. HEIR pairs human speech, measured gaze, and egocentric video with robot observations and teleoperated navigation-manipulation trajectories, while each session preserves successive episodes, with each request fulfilled through multiple execution subtasks. The captured data, together with human-authored annotations, support the study of intent grounding, execution planning, and task completion within a unified interaction setting. To demonstrate the value of HEIR, we conduct extensive offline experiments to evaluate how models benefit from additional human-centric context across these tasks, together with real-robot experiments that assess execution task completion. HEIR thus provides a valuable resource for studying user-centric robot interaction in naturalistic household environments. Our demo and dataset are available online.

</details>


### [460] [ProtoVis-Nav: Prototypical Visual Imagination for Visual-Spatial Aligned UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17330)
*Guangyuan Sui,Yunzhong Hou,Chi Harold Liu*

Abstract: Vision language navigation (VLN) is very challenging for Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) since small errors in 3D motion prediction can lead to navigation errors and collisions. Unable to fathom execution outcomes in 3D environments, existing methods often struggle to predict accurate navigation waypoints from language instructions. To solve this, we introduce the Prototypical Visual Imagination framework, shortened as ProtoVis-Nav, which forces the model to imagine an outcome-associated local visual representation for the intended next UAV movement. Specifically, we predict feature prototypes and use their linear combination to estimate the visual feature of the target region. In this manner, the VLN model first imagines the local visual representation of the next intended location, and then uses predicted prototypes as context for generating the final action, promoting alignment between visual context and waypoint outputs. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the ProtoVis-Nav framework and show substantial improvements over existing baselines.

</details>

### [461] [Learning of LPV Models With Linear Fractional Representations: Initialization, Optimization, and Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14668)

Abstract: Linear Parameter-Varying (LPV) models with Linear Fractional Representation (LFR) can describe a wide class of nonlinear systems with a control-friendly structure. However, identifying these models together with their scheduling map from input-output data requires solving a non-convex optimization problem that is highly sensitive to initialization. Consequently, existing methods often rely on many random initializations. Instead, our work proposes an initialization strategy based on the Best Linear Approximator (BLA). Furthermore, motivated by an analysis of the impact of rational-dependency LPV-LFR models on the conditioning of the non-convex cost, two multiple shooting methods that improve the conditioning, unconstrained multiple shooting (UMS) and encoder-based multiple shooting (EBMS), are compared. The initialization and multiple shooting methods are evaluated on a nonlinear mass-spring-damper example, showing that BLA initialization consistently yields accurate results, whereas random initialization has a large spread. The results also show that both UMS and EBMS reduce the spread for randomly initialized models. UMS is simple to tune and well suited to few sub-records, whereas EBMS can exploit many short sub-records to further improve conditioning, at the cost of training an encoder and tuning additional hyperparameters.

</details>

Abstract: Optimal allocation of generators is a key challenge in power systems to achieve economical dispatch while satisfying operational constraints. Conventional optimization techniques can be computationally expensive, limiting their applicability in real-time environments, and neural networks often require offline retraining under new operating conditions. This paper proposes a three-stage unsupervised online physics-informed neural network (PINN) framework for optimal power flow (OPF). Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed framework satisfies load demand and system constraints while achieving a $43,665 lower operating cost than fmincon-based optimization under the same operating conditions. The training process analysis further demonstrate the framework's ability to adapt its parameters online to variations in load demand and renewable generation in real time.

Abstract: The growth of large data centers raises questions about both supply adequacy and the suitability of load representations used in connection studies. This paper critically reviews the U.S. demand, generation, and transmission outlook through 2030 and examines the implications for transmission expansion planning (TEP). Comparison of public forecasts highlights distinctions among annual average demand, peak demand, and grid connection capacity. National generating-capacity totals cannot establish a dependable surplus because resource availability, regional adequacy, transmission constraints, and project timing determine supply capability. Recent grid events and published dynamic studies demonstrate that faults can trigger substantial data-center transfers to backup supply, followed by delayed or coordinated demand recovery. These behaviors require appropriate representation within the conventional planning framework, alongside assessment of the requested maximum grid import. The selected literature reveals limited integration of validated data-center dynamic models into the technical screening and economic comparison of expansion alternatives. The review identifies the need to establish whether fault response and recovery change feasible connection capacity, required reinforcement, or the preferred transmission investment.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes an inductively assisted N:1 switched-capacitor ladder buck (D-SCLB) converter that replaces the output-side switch in the bottom half-bridge of an N:1 SCLB with a small inductor placed in parallel with the ladder capacitors during the demagnetization phase. The flying capacitors share the load current delivery, lowering the average inductor current to (1-M)Iout and enabling continuous regulation with M=D/(N-1+D). A closed-form output-impedance model, valid for arbitrary N under both optimal and uniform switch sizing, is derived and compared against SC, SCLB, FCMC, and DPSC topologies. For a 4:1 48V-to-PoL design, the D-SCLB reduces the average inductor current by up to 20% and improves the equivalent output impedance by up to 30% relative to the SCLB. Two hardware prototypes on a low-cost two-layer PCB achieve a peak efficiency of 96.2%, with the D-SCLB using 49% smaller inductor volume than the SCLB at comparable efficiency. Model predictions agree with measurements to within 1.5% under heavy load.

Abstract: Graph-based observability analysis runs in seconds where Lie-derivative tests take hours, but it answers only one question: is the system observable or not. It cannot say how well a given set of measurements supports state reconstruction. This paper extends the graph-based approach with symbolic observability coefficients computed from a symbolic Jacobian of the state-space equations, in which every dependency is classified as constant, nonlinear polynomial, or rational. We show that the coefficient tracks the analytical observability determinant of the Lie-derivative approach: it is zero when the determinant vanishes, lies strictly between zero and one when the determinant depends on the state, and equals one when the determinant is a nonzero constant. We apply the method to a seven-state synchronous machine and to the 21-state Western System Coordinating Council nine-bus system under several synchrophasor measurement configurations. On the nine-bus system, the coefficient grows from 0.0952 with one PMU to 0.1905 with two and 0.2857 with three, and the whole analysis runs in under five seconds. Configurations with the same number of PMUs receive the same coefficient, yet an unscented Kalman filter estimates the states with different accuracy for each. The symbolic coefficient therefore grades measurement sets beyond identifying root strongly connected components, while telling PMU locations apart still requires the numerical parameters of the system.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a distributed inverse-optimal fixed-time integral reinforcement learning framework for unknown nonlinear leader-follower multi-agent systems. The proposed method first reconstructs the unknown agent dynamics from finite integral data. Based on the identified dynamics, a distributed inverse-optimal formulation is constructed by prescribing a desired fixed-time value function and recovering a compatible running cost. A critic-only integral reinforcement learning architecture is then developed using current integral data and finitely informative experience-replay data, thereby avoiding the persistent-excitation requirement. Practical fixed-time convergence of the critic-weight errors is established in the presence of identification and function-approximation residuals. Finally, a network-level composite Lyapunov analysis proves practical fixed-time leader-following consensus of the true unknown nonlinear multi-agent system. Numerical simulations are provided to validate the developed theory.

Abstract: Real-time optimal power flow (RT-OPF) requires fast and reliable decision-making under time-varying operating conditions. However, many practical operating preferences and safety related requirements are difficult to encode using conventional numerical rewards alone, which limits the effectiveness and controllability of deep reinforcement learning (DRL) for RT-OPF. To address this issue, this paper proposes an LLM-enhanced DRL framework with human-in-the-loop (HITL) for RT-OPF. The proposed method augments the physics-based reward with sparse language evaluative feedback during offline training, so that semantic operational requirements can be incorporated into policy learning. To improve reliability in RT-OPF, a selective HITL calibration mechanism is further introduced to inspect and correct questionable LLM judgments before they continuously shape the learning process. Case studies on the IEEE 30-bus and IEEE 118-bus systems show that LLM feedback improves policy safety over vanilla TD3, while further HITL calibration achieves the best overall performance and more stable training behavior. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining language guidance and expert calibration for RT-OPF.

</details>

Abstract: Coordinating transmission networks, distribution networks, and microgrids enables distributed flexibility to support renewable energy integration and low-carbon operation. However, heterogeneous resources, cross-layer coupling, and varying operating requirements complicate the selection of models and coordination schemes that are both physically adequate and computationally tractable. This paper proposes TDMOpt, a LLM-driven multi-agent framework that replaces fixed modeling configurations with adaptive model composition. A layered component library provides executable network, resource, objective, constraint, and coordination models. Guided by retrieved physics and component knowledge, the agents infer specific modeling requirements and jointly select compatible components across layers according to operating conditions and computational budgets. The model compositions undergo independent AC power flow validation, since feasibility in a simplified formulation does not ensure compliance with physical network limits. A refinement mechanism interprets numerical feedback to identify modeling requirements for reconsideration and revise relevant component selections. Case studies on different scale systems illustrate the framework's ability to adapt model combinations and obtain validated dispatch solutions through targeted refinement under varying operating conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Simulated Annealing (SA) is a widely used stochastic optimization algorithm. It is of great interest to understand how its ability to escape local minima at a fixed temperature depends on geometry and dimension. In prior work, we derived closed-form expressions for the expected basin escape time of a constant-temperature SA process on a one-dimensional piecewise-linear landscape. In this paper, we extend the analysis to $D$-dimensional cubic basins defined by the infinity-norm energy $V(x)=\|x\|_\infty$. The walk proposes nearest-neighbor moves uniformly and escapes upon first reaching the outer shell, that is, any site with $\|x\|_\infty=N$. We construct a one-dimensional birth-death shell-quotient chain by collapsing each concentric shell in the Metropolis electrical network to a single node while retaining its total stationary mass. We derive a closed-form expression for the mean escape time $Q_D(N)$ of this shell-quotient chain as a function of the acceptance probability $p$, the basin depth $N$, and the dimension $D$. For fixed $D$ and fixed $0<p<1$, the quotient escape time scales as $Θ(α^N/N^{D-1})$ as $N\to\infty$, where $α=1/p$. Relative to $D=1$, this gives an asymptotic speedup of order $N^{D-1}$ within this family of cubic basins. We prove that the true Metropolis mean escape time $H_D(N)$, without shell averaging, has the same $Θ(α^N/N^{D-1})$ scaling, establishing a lower bound via Rayleigh monotonicity and a matching upper bound through an explicit low-energy flow construction using Thomson's principle. Numerical solutions that exploit the walk's symmetries quantify the discrepancy between the quotient and true-chain means for $D=1,2,3$.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies first-order hyperbolic partial integro-differential equation (PIDE) and ordinary differential equation (ODE) cascades with distributed interconnection and an arbitrarily large known constant input delay. Within the backstepping--forwarding framework, a forwarding-type transformation is designed to reduce the distributed interconnection to an equivalent boundary-coupled Dirichlet cascade without requiring the PIDE to be open-loop stable. A subsequent backstepping design stabilizes the PIDE and the ODE while compensating for the input delay. Incompatible boundary conditions in the delay-compensation kernel equations may produce discontinuities along characteristic lines. We determine their jump locations and amplitudes and prove that the interface terms cancel when deriving the target equations, so the kernel discontinuities do not change the target system. Closed-loop exponential stability is established without additional plant restrictions to ensure kernel continuity. A numerical example illustrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

</details>

Abstract: Controllers designed from nominal models often degrade on mechanical systems with nonlinearities and parametric variations, such as automotive powertrains with backlash. Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) with domain randomization can acquire robustness to such model discrepancies, but wide randomization destabilizes training and yields conservative policies, and experimental transfer of such policies has rarely been demonstrated in vibration control. This paper presents model-based controller assisted domain randomization (MBCADR), a framework that integrates model-based control into domain-randomized DRL. An $H_{2}$ controller designed from the nominal model supplies a baseline action, while a residual policy trained with twin delayed deep deterministic policy gradient under randomized dynamics compensates for nonlinearities and uncertainties. This model-based assistance relieves the agent of synthesizing the entire control input, thereby stabilizing training under extensive randomization. For each realization of the uncertain parameters, the augmented plant--controller state is shown to be a Markov state of the closed loop, so that domain-randomized training is rigorously formulated as policy optimization over a parameterized family of Markov decision processes. A Lyapunov-based stability analysis further derives a forward-invariant set and an explicit ultimate bound, in which the learned action enters only through its saturation level. Monte Carlo simulations with multiple training seeds show that MBCADR reduces performance variability compared with either component alone. Experiments on a powertrain test bench with backlash, using the simulation-trained policy on an embedded processor without further tuning, confirm successful sim-to-real transfer.

</details>

However, models trained on limited fabricated devices generalize poorly to unmeasured design regions because of process-induced variations, while finite-element method (FEM) simulations, although inexpensive, exhibit systematic offsets from measurements. To predict device performance before physical prototyping, this work presents a simulation-compensated transfer-learning framework that uses computationally efficient 2.5-D FEM simulations as physics-based anchors and learns their calibration to the measurement domain. Using 4,236 measured lithium tantalate (LiTaO3) SAW resonators and 816 unique simulated configurations, five transfer strategies are systematically benchmarked under seven out-of-distribution (OOD) protocols spanning frequency, geometry, and material shifts. The proposed framework reduces OOD prediction error by 83.54% on average (up to 97.57%) relative to the Measurement-only baseline, with the best strategy selected per case, and raises the pooled R2 of frequency prediction to 1.000, offering an economically scalable route toward physics-guided, fabrication-aware machine learning for microelectromechanical-system (MEMS) acoustic-device design.

Abstract: Learning-to-optimise methods for security-constrained optimal power flow (SCOPF) predict the security constraints that bind on each instance and solve only the reduced problem, with reported speed-ups of two to four orders of magnitude over the full problem. We ask whether learned selection beats the strongest learning-free alternative: a fixed umbrella set enforcing every constraint observed to bind in training. Our criterion, the price of a spare, answers this in advance: an enforced but inactive constraint is free, so the umbrella is exactly optimal wherever feasible, and learning can pay only where a spare decision carries a price. We test it systematically, including every PGLib-OPF network up to 10,000 buses. For SCOPF on a fixed network the umbrella is never beaten: its median size is five constraints across 43 networks, with no size trend, and our selector, while reproducing speed-ups of published magnitude (about x80,000 at case500), gains at most 0.25 ms over the umbrella and violates constraints on 4-5% of instances. Configuration diversity can break the umbrella's coverage on a small, densely meshed network but not at 2,000 buses, and we certify coverage with conformal risk control. In unit commitment, where a spare has a price, a static commitment loses 4-12% out of sample, and a predict-repair-reduce pipeline recovers it (cost gap 0.2-2.9%, fully feasible, 16-161x faster than the MILP). In our experiments, whether learning pays depends not on problem size but on whether a spare carries a price.

</details>
